How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote and ran `invert.py`. It recovers the model with **SNR = 30.40 dB**, compared with 13.26 dB for a plain damped least-squares inversion. The last line it prints is `SNR_DB=30.39608352879786`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Regularisation:** total variation, meaning a penalty on the absolute size of the model's first derivative. Your model is piecewise constant, and this penalty favours flat layers with sharp steps. Smoothing or plain damping can't produce sharp steps, so they leave rounded edges and ripples (the blue curve in the plot).
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`, with `FirstDerivative(kind="forward", edge=False)` as the penalised term. Each outer iteration runs 5 inner iterations of up to 30 LSQR steps.
- **Regularisation weight:** I fixed `eps=1` and tried `mu` = 3, 5, 10, 20, 40 in turn. The script keeps the first value whose data misfit reaches the expected noise level, 0.02·√200 ≈ 0.283. That is `mu=10`, with a misfit of 0.275. The selection never uses `xtrue`; it's used only to compute the final SNR. I also checked first that the convolution operator's adjoint is correct (PyLops's `dottest`).

**How well it worked**
- **Fit to the data:** the relative misfit is 0.0234, against a noise level of 0.0241. The data are fitted down to the noise but not past it, so the noise isn't being fitted.
- **Model:** the plot in `invert.png` shows the recovered model (red) with sharp steps at the right positions and the right values (1, −0.5, 2), and no ringing. The leftover error is mostly one or two samples of rounding at each step.
- **Convergence:** the solver used all 300 outer iterations rather than stopping early on its tolerance. The cost fell from 10.27 to 7.35. In my exploratory runs, going from 100 to 300 iterations moved the SNR by under 1 dB, so the result is effectively stable.
- **Runtime:** about 10 s.

**Caveats**
- In my exploratory sweeps, where I scored settings against the true model, the best was about 31.1 dB at a slightly different weight. The discrepancy principle got within about 1 dB of that, and it works on real data where you don't have a true model.
- Total variation slightly shrinks the contrast between layers. If you need the layer values to be exact, the next step is to re-fit them by least squares with the detected step positions held fixed.

The script also saves the recovered model to `xinv.npy` and the plot to `invert.png`, both in the current folder.

"""Deblurring of a 1-D blocky (layered) model with total-variation regularisation.

Solver: split Bregman (pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman) with an L1 norm
on the first derivative of the model (TV), because the true model is piecewise
constant. The TV weight (via ``mu``) is selected with the discrepancy principle,
i.e. the data residual should match the known noise level - xtrue is NOT used
for parameter selection, only for the final quality metric.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

assert dottest(Op, n, n)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- baseline
# Damped least squares (zero-order Tikhonov) for comparison only
xls = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=50, damp=1e-1)[0]

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- TV inversion
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=False)
eps = 1.0  # split-Bregman coupling / TV weight (effective TV weight ~ eps / mu)
niter_outer, niter_inner = 300, 5
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)  # expected ||y - Op xtrue||


def tv_invert(mu):
    xinv, iiter, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=niter_outer, niter_inner=niter_inner,
        mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps], tol=1e-8, tau=1.0, iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,
    )
    return xinv, iiter, cost


# Discrepancy principle: smallest data weight mu (strongest TV) whose residual
# drops to the noise level
results = {}
for mu in [3.0, 5.0, 10.0, 20.0, 40.0]:
    xinv, iiter, cost = tv_invert(mu)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv)
    results[mu] = (xinv, iiter, cost, res)
    print(f"mu={mu:5.1f}  ||r||={res:.4f}  (target {target:.4f})")
    if res <= target:
        break
mu_sel = mu
xinv, iiter, cost, res = results[mu_sel]

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- report
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"Selected mu={mu_sel}, eps={eps}, outer iterations={iiter}")
print(f"cost: first={cost[0]:.4e} last={cost[-1]:.4e}")
print(f"Relative data residual ||y-Op x||/||y|| = {res / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f} "
      f"(noise level {target / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f})")
print(f"Baseline damped LSQR SNR = {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
try:
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    fig, axs = plt.subplots(2, 1, figsize=(8, 6))
    axs[0].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=3, label="true")
    axs[0].plot(xls, "b", label="damped LSQR")
    axs[0].plot(xinv, "r", label="TV (split Bregman)")
    axs[0].legend(); axs[0].set_title("Model")
    axs[1].plot(y, "k", label="data")
    axs[1].plot(Op @ xinv, "r", label="modelled")
    axs[1].plot(y - Op @ xinv, "g", label="residual")
    axs[1].legend(); axs[1].set_title("Data")
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("invert.png", dpi=120)
except ImportError:
    pass

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it in about 10 s. On your synthetic the recovered model scores **SNR = 30.5 dB** against the truth (last line `SNR_DB=30.54968044995574`).

**What I chose**
- **Total-variation regularisation** (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`, an L1 penalty on `FirstDerivative`). Your model is piecewise-constant, so penalising jumps keeps the layer edges sharp while suppressing noise.
- **Weight picked from the noise level, not the truth.** The script tries 13 TV weights from 1e-3 to 1 and keeps the largest one whose data misfit is still within the noise level (σ·√n ≈ 0.283). That gives eps ≈ 0.316. Choosing this way means a good score isn't just the result of tuning against `xtrue`.
- **Comparison inversion:** a standard smoothness-regularised inversion (second-derivative penalty, `regularized_inversion`) on the same data.

**How well it worked**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Smoothness-regularised baseline | 13.3 dB |
| **TV, eps = 0.316** | **30.5 dB** |

- Data misfit ‖y − Op x‖/‖y‖ is 0.0234, against a noise level of 0.0241, so the result fits the data about as closely as the noise allows without fitting the noise.
- The largest single-sample error is 0.25, and it sits at the block edges.
- Across the sweep, the selected weight also happened to give the best SNR: 30.5 dB, compared with 27.2 dB one grid step lower and 25.3 dB one step higher.
- The adjoint check on the blur operator passes.

**Caveat on convergence:** the solver used all 100 of its outer iterations rather than stopping on its tolerance. When I re-ran with 300 iterations, the SNR moved by less than 1 dB (about 30.1 dB at eps = 0.3), so the answer is stable, but strictly it hasn't fully converged.

The script also saves `xtrue`, `y`, the TV result, the baseline result and the chosen weight to `invert_results.npz`.

**Possible next steps:**
- A finer search over the weight between about 0.2 and 0.5.
- Adjusting the solver's internal weight (`mu`) and iteration counts.
- If you need hard bounds on the model values, a constrained TV solve with PyProximal would handle that.

"""TV (split-Bregman) deconvolution of a blocky 1-D model blurred by a Gaussian kernel.

The TV weight is chosen with the discrepancy principle (data residual ~ noise
level, sigma = 0.02 known), so the choice does not use the true model.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

pylops.utils.dottest(Op, n, n, raiseerror=True)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- baseline: L2 smoothness
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)
x_l2 = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[0.5], **dict(damp=0, iter_lim=200, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)
)[0]

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- TV via split Bregman
D1 = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
mu = 1.0  # weight on the data term; TV strength is set by eps relative to mu


def tv_invert(eps):
    xinv, niter_out, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D1], niter_outer=100, niter_inner=10, mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps],
        tol=1e-6, tau=1.0, iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,
    )
    return xinv, niter_out, cost


target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)  # expected ||y - Op x|| for the right regularisation
epss = np.logspace(-3, 0, 13)
results = []
print(f"{'eps':>9} {'||r||':>8} {'target':>8} {'SNR(dB)':>8}")
for eps in epss:
    xinv, nout, cost = tv_invert(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv)
    results.append((eps, res, xinv, nout, cost))
    print(f"{eps:9.2e} {res:8.4f} {target:8.4f} {snr(xtrue, xinv):8.2f}")

# discrepancy principle: largest eps whose residual does not exceed the noise level
ok = [r for r in results if r[1] <= target]
eps_sel, res_sel, xinv, nout, cost = ok[-1] if ok else results[0]

print(f"\nL2 (2nd-derivative) baseline: SNR = {snr(xtrue, x_l2):.2f} dB")
print(f"Selected TV eps = {eps_sel:.3e} (discrepancy principle)")
print(f"  outer iterations used: {nout}, cost first/last: {cost[0]:.3e} / {cost[-1]:.3e}")
print(f"  ||y - Op x|| / ||y|| = {res_sel / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f} "
      f"(noise level {target / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f})")
print(f"  max |x - xtrue| = {np.abs(xinv - xtrue).max():.3f}")

np.savez("invert_results.npz", xtrue=xtrue, y=y, xinv=xinv, x_l2=x_l2, eps=eps_sel)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it in about 10 s. On your synthetic the recovered model scores **SNR = 30.5 dB** against the truth (last line `SNR_DB=30.54968044995574`).

**What I chose**
- **Total-variation regularisation** (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`, an L1 penalty on `FirstDerivative`). Your model is piecewise-constant, so penalising jumps keeps the layer edges sharp while suppressing noise.
- **Weight picked from the noise level, not the truth.** The script tries 13 TV weights from 1e-3 to 1 and keeps the largest one whose data misfit is still within the noise level (σ·√n ≈ 0.283). That gives eps ≈ 0.316. Choosing this way means a good score isn't just the result of tuning against `xtrue`.
- **Comparison inversion:** a standard smoothness-regularised inversion (second-derivative penalty, `regularized_inversion`) on the same data.

**How well it worked**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Smoothness-regularised baseline | 13.3 dB |
| **TV, eps = 0.316** | **30.5 dB** |

- Data misfit ‖y − Op x‖/‖y‖ is 0.0234, against a noise level of 0.0241, so the result fits the data about as closely as the noise allows without fitting the noise.
- The largest single-sample error is 0.25, and it sits at the block edges.
- Across the sweep, the selected weight also happened to give the best SNR: 30.5 dB, compared with 27.2 dB one grid step lower and 25.3 dB one step higher.
- The adjoint check on the blur operator passes.

**Caveat on convergence:** the solver used all 100 of its outer iterations rather than stopping on its tolerance. When I re-ran with 300 iterations, the SNR moved by less than 1 dB (about 30.1 dB at eps = 0.3), so the answer is stable, but strictly it hasn't fully converged.

The script also saves `xtrue`, `y`, the TV result, the baseline result and the chosen weight to `invert_results.npz`.

**Possible next steps:**
- A finer search over the weight between about 0.2 and 0.5.
- Adjusting the solver's internal weight (`mu`) and iteration counts.
- If you need hard bounds on the model values, a constrained TV solve with PyProximal would handle that.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers the model at **29.9 dB SNR**, compared with 11.0 dB for a plain unregularised LSQR inversion. The last line printed is `SNR_DB=29.89392041269815`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Solver:** `splitbregman` with total-variation regularisation, meaning an L1 penalty on `FirstDerivative`. Your model is made of flat blocks with sharp jumps, and that penalty favours exactly that. Smoothing (L2) regularisation would round off the edges, and plain least squares turns the noise into oscillations.
- **Settings:** `mu=1`, 100 outer and 10 inner iterations, and the inner LSQR is limited to 30 iterations.
- **Regularisation weight:** I picked it from the noise level, not from the true model. The script tries a range of weights and keeps the one whose data misfit (RMS) is closest to the noise level of 0.02. That selected `eps=0.35`, with a residual RMS of 0.0199. This method also works on real data, where you don't know the true model.
- **Checks:** the dot-test on the blur operator passes, so the operator and its adjoint are consistent. The cost fell from 9.3 to 7.0. The script also saves the recovered model to `xinv.npy`.

**How good the result is**
- In earlier test runs, weights between about 0.25 and 0.35 all gave roughly 30–31 dB. The best I saw was about 31 dB at `eps=0.25`, but choosing that would have meant using the true model, which you won't have with real data. So the automatic choice gives up very little.
- The run stopped at the 100-iteration limit, not on its tolerance. With 300 iterations the score stays around 29–30 dB, so more iterations don't help.

The script doesn't make a plot. If you want to tune further, the main lever is the ratio of `eps` to `mu`.

"""Deblurring of a 1-D blocky (piecewise-constant) layered model with PyLops.

Solver: split-Bregman with total-variation (L1 on the first derivative)
regularisation, because the model is piecewise constant. The TV weight is
picked with the discrepancy principle (data residual RMS ~ noise sigma), so the
true model is used only for the final quality metric.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# synthetic model
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

# Gaussian blur operator
t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, n, n)

# noisy data
sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# reference: unregularised least squares (early stopping only)
xlsqr = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=50)[0]
print(f"LSQR (no regularisation)  SNR = {snr(xtrue, xlsqr):.2f} dB")

# TV-regularised inversion with split-Bregman
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)
mu = 1.0
niter_outer, niter_inner = 100, 10


def tv_invert(eps):
    xinv, iiter, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=niter_outer, niter_inner=niter_inner,
        mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps], tol=1e-8, tau=1.0,
        iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,  # inner lsqr settings
    )
    return xinv, iiter, cost


# discrepancy principle: choose eps whose residual RMS is closest to sigma
epss = [0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.25, 0.3, 0.35, 0.4, 0.5, 1.0]
results = []
for eps in epss:
    xinv, iiter, cost = tv_invert(eps)
    rms = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv) / np.sqrt(n)
    results.append((eps, xinv, iiter, cost, rms))
    print(f"eps={eps:5.2f}  residual RMS={rms:.4f} (sigma={sigma})")

eps, xinv, iiter, cost, rms = min(results, key=lambda r: abs(r[4] - sigma))
print(f"Chosen eps={eps} (mu={mu}), outer iterations={iiter}, "
      f"cost {cost[0]:.3e} -> {cost[-1]:.3e}")
print(f"Relative data residual ||y-Op x||/||y|| = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f}")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers the model at **29.9 dB SNR**, compared with 11.0 dB for a plain unregularised LSQR inversion. The last line printed is `SNR_DB=29.89392041269815`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Solver:** `splitbregman` with total-variation regularisation, meaning an L1 penalty on `FirstDerivative`. Your model is made of flat blocks with sharp jumps, and that penalty favours exactly that. Smoothing (L2) regularisation would round off the edges, and plain least squares turns the noise into oscillations.
- **Settings:** `mu=1`, 100 outer and 10 inner iterations, and the inner LSQR is limited to 30 iterations.
- **Regularisation weight:** I picked it from the noise level, not from the true model. The script tries a range of weights and keeps the one whose data misfit (RMS) is closest to the noise level of 0.02. That selected `eps=0.35`, with a residual RMS of 0.0199. This method also works on real data, where you don't know the true model.
- **Checks:** the dot-test on the blur operator passes, so the operator and its adjoint are consistent. The cost fell from 9.3 to 7.0. The script also saves the recovered model to `xinv.npy`.

**How good the result is**
- In earlier test runs, weights between about 0.25 and 0.35 all gave roughly 30–31 dB. The best I saw was about 31 dB at `eps=0.25`, but choosing that would have meant using the true model, which you won't have with real data. So the automatic choice gives up very little.
- The run stopped at the 100-iteration limit, not on its tolerance. With 300 iterations the score stays around 29–30 dB, so more iterations don't help.

The script doesn't make a plot. If you want to tune further, the main lever is the ratio of `eps` to `mu`.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D blocky-model deblurring with PyLops using Total-Variation regularisation.

The true model is piecewise constant, so we solve

    min_x  mu/2 ||y - Op x||_2^2 + eps ||D x||_1

(D = first derivative) with Split-Bregman. The TV weight `eps` is chosen
without looking at the true model, via Morozov's discrepancy principle:
pick the eps whose data residual ||y - Op x|| is closest to the expected
noise level sigma * sqrt(n).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- synthetic model -------------------------------------------------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

# --- blurring operator -----------------------------------------------------
t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

# --- data ------------------------------------------------------------------
sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# --- baseline: Tikhonov (L2) smoothing, for comparison ---------------------
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="backward")
xl2 = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[3e-2], iter_lim=500
)[0]
print(f"L2-smoothing baseline        : SNR = {snr(xtrue, xl2):6.2f} dB")


# --- TV inversion with Split-Bregman ---------------------------------------
def tv_invert(eps, mu=1.0):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D],
        niter_outer=300, niter_inner=5,
        mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps], tau=1.0, tol=1e-8,
        iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,
    )[0]


target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)  # expected ||noise||
best = None
for eps in [0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.25, 0.3, 0.35, 0.4, 0.5, 0.7]:
    x = tv_invert(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)
    print(f"TV eps={eps:4.2f}: residual = {res:.3f} (target {target:.3f})")
    if best is None or abs(res - target) < abs(best[2] - target):
        best = (eps, x, res)

eps, xinv, res = best
print(f"Selected eps = {eps} by discrepancy principle (residual {res:.3f})")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

"""TV-regularised deconvolution of a blocky 1-D model with PyLops.

Solves  min_x  mu/2 ||y - Op x||_2^2 + lam ||D x||_1   (Split-Bregman),
with D a first derivative, i.e. total-variation regularisation, which favours
piecewise-constant models. The TV weight lam/mu is chosen with Morozov's
discrepancy principle (data residual ~ noise level), so the true model is
only used for the final quality metric.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic -----------------------------------------------------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---- inversion -----------------------------------------------------------
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
mu = 2.0


def tv_invert(weight):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=300, niter_inner=5, mu=mu,
        epsRL1s=[weight * mu], tol=1e-8, tau=1.0, show=False,
        **dict(iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10),
    )[0]


# Discrepancy principle: find TV weight whose residual norm ~= sigma*sqrt(n).
# Residual grows monotonically with the weight, so bisect in log-space.
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
lo, hi = np.log(0.01), np.log(1.0)
for _ in range(12):
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ tv_invert(np.exp(mid)))
    lo, hi = (mid, hi) if res < target else (lo, mid)
weight = np.exp(0.5 * (lo + hi))
xinv = tv_invert(weight)

# Least-squares baseline for comparison (smooth Tikhonov, same D).
xls = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[np.sqrt(3e-3)], **dict(iter_lim=500)
)[0]

print(f"TV weight (lam/mu)      : {weight:.4f}")
print(f"Residual / target       : {np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv):.4f} / {target:.4f}")
print(f"SNR Tikhonov baseline   : {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR data-as-model (y)   : {snr(xtrue, y):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

"""Blocky 1-D deconvolution with PyLops: total-variation (TV) regularised inversion.

The model is piecewise constant, so we promote sparsity of its first derivative
(anisotropic TV) and solve with Split-Bregman:

    min_x  mu/2 ||y - Op x||_2^2 + ||D x||_1

The trade-off mu is chosen *without* using the true model, via Morozov's
discrepancy principle: pick the mu whose data residual RMS is closest to the
known noise standard deviation (0.02).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------- synthetic ----------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---------------- baseline: L2 (Tikhonov on first derivative) ----------------
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)
xl2 = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[0.1], iter_lim=500
)[0]
print(f"L2-smooth baseline:   SNR = {snr(xtrue, xl2):6.2f} dB")


# ---------------- TV inversion via Split-Bregman ----------------
def tv_invert(mu):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D],
        niter_outer=100, niter_inner=5,
        mu=mu, epsRL1s=[1.0], tol=1e-6, tau=1.0,
        iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,
    )[0]


# discrepancy principle: residual RMS ~ sigma
mus = np.logspace(0, 2, 13)
best = None
for mu in mus:
    x = tv_invert(mu)
    rms = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x) / np.sqrt(n)
    print(f"  mu = {mu:7.2f}   residual RMS = {rms:.4f}")
    if best is None or abs(rms - sigma) < abs(best[2] - sigma):
        best = (mu, x, rms)

mu, xinv, rms = best
print(f"Selected mu = {mu:.2f} (residual RMS {rms:.4f} vs noise sigma {sigma})")
print(f"TV (Split-Bregman):   SNR = {snr(xtrue, xinv):6.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The recovered model has an SNR of **31.09 dB** against the true model. The script's last line is `SNR_DB=31.090105852332343`.

**What I chose**
- **Regularisation: total variation (TV).** Your model is made of flat blocks, so I penalised the L1 norm of the model's first derivative (`pylops.FirstDerivative`). This lets the model have a few sharp jumps but keeps it flat in between, which suits a layered earth. Smooth L2 regularisation would round off the layer edges.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`, minimising `mu/2·||y − Op x||² + ||D x||₁`, with 100 outer and 5 inner iterations.
- **Choosing the trade-off `mu` without using the true model:** I used the discrepancy principle. The script tries 13 values of `mu` from 1 to 100 and picks the one where the mismatch to the data (residual RMS) is closest to the known noise level of 0.02. It picked `mu = 10`, with a residual RMS of 0.0203. `xtrue` is only used for the final SNR, so the method would work the same on real data where you know the noise level.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR (dB) |
|---|---|
| Smooth L2 baseline (derivative penalty, eps = 0.1) | 13.7 |
| **TV / Split-Bregman, mu = 10** | **31.1** |

In an earlier test run I also scored a few values of `mu` against the true model. The value the discrepancy principle picks gave the highest SNR of those I tried: 26.9 dB at `mu` = 3, 29.4 dB at 30 and 26.2 dB at 100. So the automatic choice is close to the best possible for this setup. The L2 baseline is there for comparison only; the script runs it once with a fixed setting and doesn't tune it.

  • the blur is a Gaussian kernel h = exp(-0.5 * (t / 5)**2) for integer t from −20 to 20, normalised to sum to 1 and centred (pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(200, h=h, offset=20));

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The inversion recovers the model with an SNR of **63.8 dB**. A plain least-squares inversion of the same data gets only **2.5 dB**.

**What I chose and why**

The "odd readings" are large spikes on about 10% of the data. Ordinary least squares fits them as if they were real, and they wreck the result. The inversion only uses `A` and `y`; the script keeps your generation code at the top just to create the test data, and uses `xtrue` only for the final SNR line.

1. **Plain `lsqr` as a baseline.** It converged in 23 iterations, but 77% of the data was left unexplained and the SNR was 2.49 dB, so the bad readings dominate it.
2. **Main step: `irls` with `kind="data"`.** This minimises the absolute size of the misfits instead of their squares. Each outer step down-weights readings with large misfits, so the spikes are progressively ignored. It is PyLops' standard solver for outlier-robust inversion.
   - The one tuning setting, `epsR`, is set from the data. I estimated the noise level from the baseline misfits using the median absolute deviation, which outliers don't distort, and set `epsR` a thousand times smaller (5.5e-3).
   - It converged in 27 of the 50 allowed outer iterations.
3. **Flag and refit.** After IRLS, the estimated noise level is about **0.0103**, which matches the true 0.01. Readings whose misfit is more than 5 times that are flagged: **exactly 30**, the same number as the injected spikes. I then re-ran `lsqr` using only the 270 good readings, which gives the cleanest final answer.

**How well it worked**

| | `lsqr` | IRLS + refit on good readings |
|---|---|---|
| SNR against true model | 2.49 dB | **63.85 dB** |
| Unexplained fraction of data | 0.77 | 1.3e-3 (good readings only) |

The leftover misfit on the good readings is at the noise level, so it is not over-fitting.

The script saves the model to `xinv.npy`, and its last line prints `SNR_DB=63.8455`.

**For your real survey**

- **Outlier cut-off:** the 5× noise threshold can be adjusted. You can also print the indices in `flagged` to see which sensors look faulty.
- **If outliers are a large share of your data** (much more than about 30%), check the result more carefully.
- **If your real `A` is badly conditioned**, the small Tikhonov damping `epsI` (currently 1e-10) may need to be increased and tuned. It isn't needed here because this `A` is well conditioned (300×50, random).

"""Robust (L1-misfit) inversion of sensor data containing outlier readings.

Only ``y`` and ``A`` are used by the inversion; ``xtrue`` is used solely to
report the final SNR on this synthetic example.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic data
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# From here on only A and y are used
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)


def mad_sigma(r):
    """Robust (outlier-insensitive) estimate of the noise standard deviation."""
    return 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))


# Reference: plain L2 least squares (sensitive to outliers)
xls, istop, itn = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:3]
r_ls = y - Op @ xls
print(f"LSQR (L2):  istop={istop}, iters={itn}, "
      f"rel. residual={np.linalg.norm(r_ls) / np.linalg.norm(y):.3e}")

# Robust: IRLS with L1 data misfit, ||y - Op x||_1. Outliers get weights
# ~1/|r| and are progressively ignored. epsR stabilises weights for tiny
# residuals; set it well below the noise level estimated robustly from the
# L2 residual (MAD ignores the outliers).
epsR = 1e-3 * mad_sigma(r_ls)
xinv, nouter = irls(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, epsR=epsR, epsI=1e-10,
                    tolIRLS=1e-8, kind="data", warm=True,
                    iter_lim=200, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)
r = y - Op @ xinv
sigma = mad_sigma(r)
flagged = np.abs(r) > 5 * sigma
print(f"IRLS (L1):  outer iters={nouter}, epsR={epsR:.2e}")
print(f"  robust noise estimate (MAD of residual) = {sigma:.4f}")
print(f"  flagged outliers (|r| > 5 sigma): {flagged.sum()} readings")
print(f"  inlier rel. residual = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(r[~flagged]) / np.linalg.norm(y[~flagged]):.3e}")

# Final polish: L2 refit on inliers only (Restriction to the good readings)
R = pylops.Restriction(nobs, np.where(~flagged)[0], dtype=Op.dtype)
xref = lsqr(R @ Op, R @ y, x0=xinv, niter=100, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)[0]
xinv = xref
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)

print(f"SNR LSQR (for comparison) = {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovered the model with **SNR = 60.8 dB**, compared with 2.5 dB for plain least squares on the same data.

**What I chose and why**
- **Problem:** `A` is 300×50, so there are many more readings than unknowns and you don't need regularisation. The odd readings are a handful of very large spikes (tens of units), while the normal noise is about 0.01. Ordinary least squares spreads those spikes across the whole model.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. This uses an L1 data misfit instead of L2: it repeatedly re-solves the least-squares problem, giving each reading a weight of about 1/|residual|. Spiky readings end up with almost no influence. I used `epsR=1e-4`, which keeps the weights finite and is set well below the 0.01 noise level, along with warm starts and 50 outer iterations. Each re-solve uses scipy's `lsqr` with `iter_lim=100`.
- The inversion only sees `y` and `A`. `xtrue` is used only to compute the final SNR line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr` (baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| IRLS with L1 misfit | **60.81 dB** |

- **Residual check:** using a robust estimate of the residual spread (the median absolute deviation, MAD), the spread is 0.0097. The RMS of the residuals on the good readings is 0.0099, which matches the true 0.01 noise level, so the model fits the clean data down to the noise without overfitting.
- **Flagged readings:** 30 readings have a residual more than 5 × MAD and are flagged as outliers. That matches the 30 corrupted readings in your script, though the script doesn't check the flagged indices against `bad`. The overall relative residual is 0.86 only because the outliers dominate ‖y‖, which is expected.
- **Outputs:** the model is saved to `xinv.npy` and the flagged reading indices to `flagged.npy`. The last printed line is `SNR_DB=60.80815509039644`.

**Notes**
- IRLS ran all 50 outer iterations because `tolIRLS=1e-10` is very strict. The result is already at the noise level, so 15–20 iterations would probably be enough if speed matters on the real survey.
- If you want the cleanest result possible, one option is to drop the flagged readings and run a plain least-squares fit on the remaining ones.
- On real data, check that the 5 × MAD cut flags a plausible number of sensors. If outliers are smaller or more numerous than in this synthetic case, you may need to adjust `epsR` or the threshold.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data containing outlier readings.

Solver: IRLS with an L1 data misfit (pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls,
kind="data"). Outliers are a few large spikes in y, so an L2 misfit (lsqr)
smears them into the model; an L1 misfit down-weights them automatically.
Only y and A are used by the inversion; xtrue is used only for the final SNR.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- synthetic data (in the real survey only y and A are available) ---
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# --- sanity check on the operator ---
pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape, raiseerror=True)

# --- baseline: plain least squares (L2 misfit) ---
xls, istop, itn = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:3]
print(f"LSQR   : istop={istop}, iters={itn}, SNR={snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")

# --- robust inversion: IRLS with L1 data misfit ---
# epsR stabilises the weights 1/|r| for near-zero residuals; set it well below
# the expected noise level of the good readings (here ~0.01).
nouter = 50
xinv, nouter_used = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=nouter, epsR=1e-4, tolIRLS=1e-10,
    kind="data", warm=True, iter_lim=100,  # inner scipy lsqr iterations
)
r = y - Op @ xinv
print(f"IRLS   : outer iterations used={nouter_used}/{nouter}")

# data residual diagnostics: inliers should sit at noise level, outliers flagged
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))
flagged = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(r) > 5 * mad)
inl = np.setdiff1d(np.arange(nobs), flagged)
print(f"Robust residual scale (MAD) = {mad:.4f}")
print(f"Readings flagged as outliers (|r| > 5 MAD): {flagged.size}")
print(f"Inlier residual RMS = {np.sqrt(np.mean(r[inl] ** 2)):.4f}")
print(f"Relative residual ||y - Ax||/||y|| = {np.linalg.norm(r) / np.linalg.norm(y):.3f}")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
np.save("flagged.npy", flagged)

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovered the model with **SNR = 60.8 dB**, compared with 2.5 dB for plain least squares on the same data.

**What I chose and why**
- **Problem:** `A` is 300×50, so there are many more readings than unknowns and you don't need regularisation. The odd readings are a handful of very large spikes (tens of units), while the normal noise is about 0.01. Ordinary least squares spreads those spikes across the whole model.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. This uses an L1 data misfit instead of L2: it repeatedly re-solves the least-squares problem, giving each reading a weight of about 1/|residual|. Spiky readings end up with almost no influence. I used `epsR=1e-4`, which keeps the weights finite and is set well below the 0.01 noise level, along with warm starts and 50 outer iterations. Each re-solve uses scipy's `lsqr` with `iter_lim=100`.
- The inversion only sees `y` and `A`. `xtrue` is used only to compute the final SNR line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr` (baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| IRLS with L1 misfit | **60.81 dB** |

- **Residual check:** using a robust estimate of the residual spread (the median absolute deviation, MAD), the spread is 0.0097. The RMS of the residuals on the good readings is 0.0099, which matches the true 0.01 noise level, so the model fits the clean data down to the noise without overfitting.
- **Flagged readings:** 30 readings have a residual more than 5 × MAD and are flagged as outliers. That matches the 30 corrupted readings in your script, though the script doesn't check the flagged indices against `bad`. The overall relative residual is 0.86 only because the outliers dominate ‖y‖, which is expected.
- **Outputs:** the model is saved to `xinv.npy` and the flagged reading indices to `flagged.npy`. The last printed line is `SNR_DB=60.80815509039644`.

**Notes**
- IRLS ran all 50 outer iterations because `tolIRLS=1e-10` is very strict. The result is already at the noise level, so 15–20 iterations would probably be enough if speed matters on the real survey.
- If you want the cleanest result possible, one option is to drop the flagged readings and run a plain least-squares fit on the remaining ones.
- On real data, check that the 5 × MAD cut flags a plausible number of sensors. If outliers are smaller or more numerous than in this synthetic case, you may need to adjust `epsR` or the threshold.

It doesn't need to be told which readings are bad, and it only uses `y` and `A`. The script also runs a plain `lsqr` fit for comparison.

| `irls` (robust fit) | **60.48 dB** | 0.0066 |

  - `nouter` is the number of rounds.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers.

Only ``y`` and ``A`` are used by the inversion; ``xtrue`` is used solely to
evaluate the result (synthetic test).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic data
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# From here on only y and A (through Op) are used.
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)

# Baseline: ordinary least squares (L2 misfit) - sensitive to outliers
xls, istop, itn_ls = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:3]

# Robust: IRLS with L1 data misfit, weights 1/max(|r|, epsR) re-estimated
# at every outer iteration so outlying readings get very small weights
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, kind="data",
    epsR=1e-8, epsI=0.0, tolIRLS=1e-8, warm=True,
)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- diagnostics
res_ls = y - Op @ xls
res = y - Op @ xinv
mad = np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
sigma_robust = 1.4826 * mad
flagged = np.where(np.abs(res) > 5 * sigma_robust)[0]

print(f"LSQR : istop={istop}, iters={itn_ls}, SNR={snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"IRLS : outer iterations={nouter}")
print(f"robust residual std (MAD) = {sigma_robust:.4f}")
print(f"readings flagged as outliers (|r| > 5 sigma): {len(flagged)}")
print(f"median |residual| LSQR={np.median(np.abs(res_ls)):.4f} "
      f"IRLS={np.median(np.abs(res)):.4f}")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
np.save("outliers_idx.npy", flagged)

# Evaluation against the known model (synthetic test only)
print(f"flagged set equals injected outliers: {set(flagged) == set(bad)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovers `x` to **60.5 dB SNR**; a plain least-squares inversion of the same data gets only 2.5 dB.

**What I chose and why:** The "odd" readings are large spikes of ±20–50 sitting on top of noise of about 0.01. Least squares treats every residual as Gaussian, so a few spikes pull the whole solution off. I instead minimised the **L1 data misfit** `||y − A x||₁` with PyLops' IRLS solver (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls`, `kind="data"`). It reweights the data on each iteration, so large residuals lose influence. The inversion uses only `y` and `A`; `xtrue` is used only to compute the score at the end. Settings: 50 outer iterations, `epsR=1e-8` to stabilise the weights, and no model damping because the problem is well-overdetermined (300 × 50).

**Results:**
```
Least-squares   SNR: 2.49 dB
IRLS (L1) outer iterations: 50
Robust residual noise estimate: 0.0097
Flagged outliers: 30 (true outliers among them: 30/30)
SNR_DB=60.48740136084895
```

- **Accuracy:** 60.5 dB means the model error is about 0.1% of the model.
- **Noise estimate:** a robust estimate from the residuals (based on median absolute deviation) gives σ ≈ 0.0097, close to the true 0.01. This means the fit is at the noise level.
- **Spotting bad readings:** marking residuals above 5σ as suspect caught all 30 corrupted readings, with no false flags. On real survey data you can use this list to check which sensors misbehaved. The `bad` indices are used only to report the 30/30 match.

The IRLS used all 50 outer iterations without meeting its stopping tolerance (1e-10). The result is still accurate, so this costs nothing here.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers.

Some readings are corrupted by large spikes, so a plain least-squares (L2)
misfit would be dragged towards them. We instead minimise an L1 data misfit
||y - A x||_1 with IRLS, which down-weights large residuals automatically.
Only `y` and `A` are used by the inversion; `xtrue` is used only for scoring.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------- synthetic data (as in the real survey: only y, A known) ---
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------- inversion (uses only y and A) -----------------------------
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# Baseline: ordinary least squares (for comparison only)
xls = regularized_inversion(Op, y, [], **dict(iter_lim=200, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10))[0]

# Robust: L1 data misfit via IRLS
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op,
    y,
    x0=np.zeros(m),
    nouter=50,
    threshR=False,
    epsR=1e-8,
    epsI=0.0,
    tolIRLS=1e-10,
    kind="data",
    **dict(iter_lim=200, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10),
)[:2]

# Flag suspicious readings from the robust residuals (MAD-based, 5 sigma)
res = y - Op @ xinv
sigma = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
flagged = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(res) > 5 * sigma)

print(f"Least-squares   SNR: {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"IRLS (L1) outer iterations: {nouter}")
print(f"Robust residual noise estimate: {sigma:.4f}")
print(f"Flagged outliers: {len(flagged)} "
      f"(true outliers among them: {np.isin(flagged, bad).sum()}/{len(bad)})")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and the robust inversion recovered `x` with an SNR of **60.5 dB**. A standard least-squares fit on the same data only reached 2.5 dB.

**What I chose and why:** the odd readings are a few very large spikes on top of small noise. An ordinary least-squares fit tries to match those spikes, and they pull the whole answer off. So I used PyLops' `irls` with `kind="data"`, which fits the data with an L1 (absolute-value) misfit. On each pass it reweights every reading by the inverse of its current error, so readings that don't agree with the rest get less and less say in the answer.

**Results:**

| Method | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| LSQR (least squares, baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| **IRLS, L1 data misfit (final)** | **60.50 dB** |

- **The spikes can be found without labels.** After the inversion, I flag any reading whose error is more than 5 times the typical spread (estimated with the median absolute deviation, which spikes don't distort). That flags exactly 30 readings, and all 30 are the corrupted ones.
- **The fit is about as good as the noise allows.** The typical leftover error on the good readings is 0.0096, which matches the 0.01 noise you added. The 60 dB result means the spikes have essentially no effect on the answer.
- **It ran all 50 passes** (the cap I set) rather than meeting the stopping tolerance, which is very tight. The result still reaches the noise level.

On your real survey you can use the same flagged list to see which sensors look faulty. If you want an extra step, drop those readings and run a plain least-squares fit on the rest.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic data (as provided) ----
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---- inversion: only y and A are used from here on ----
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# Baseline: ordinary least squares (L2 misfit), sensitive to outliers
xl2 = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=200, atol=0, btol=0)[0]

# Robust: L1 data misfit via IRLS (reweights residuals, down-weighting outliers)
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, threshR=False, epsR=1e-8,
    kind="data", tolIRLS=1e-10, **dict(iter_lim=200, atol=0, btol=0),
)[:2]

# Diagnostics using only y and A: residual-based outlier flags (MAD scale)
r = y - Op @ xinv
scale = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))
flagged = np.abs(r) > 5 * scale
print(f"IRLS outer iterations: {nouter}")
print(f"Robust residual scale (MAD): {scale:.4f}")
print(f"Readings flagged as outliers: {flagged.sum()} / {nobs}")
print(f"  (synthetic check) correctly flagged: {np.isin(np.where(flagged)[0], bad).sum()} / {len(bad)}")
print(f"LSQR (L2) SNR:  {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")
print(f"IRLS (L1) SNR:  {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and the robust inversion recovered `x` with an SNR of **60.5 dB**. A standard least-squares fit on the same data only reached 2.5 dB.

**What I chose and why:** the odd readings are a few very large spikes on top of small noise. An ordinary least-squares fit tries to match those spikes, and they pull the whole answer off. So I used PyLops' `irls` with `kind="data"`, which fits the data with an L1 (absolute-value) misfit. On each pass it reweights every reading by the inverse of its current error, so readings that don't agree with the rest get less and less say in the answer.

**Results:**

| Method | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| LSQR (least squares, baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| **IRLS, L1 data misfit (final)** | **60.50 dB** |

- **Only `y` and `A` are used.** The inversion never touches `bad`, `xtrue` or the noise level. `xtrue` appears only to compute the SNR you asked for, and `bad` only for one check line in the printout.
- **The spikes can be found without labels.** After the inversion, I flag any reading whose error is more than 5 times the typical spread (estimated with the median absolute deviation, which spikes don't distort). That flags exactly 30 readings, and all 30 are the corrupted ones.
- **The fit is about as good as the noise allows.** The typical leftover error on the good readings is 0.0096, which matches the 0.01 noise you added. The 60 dB result means the spikes have essentially no effect on the answer.
- **It ran all 50 passes** (the cap I set) rather than meeting the stopping tolerance, which is very tight. The result still reaches the noise level.

On your real survey you can use the same flagged list to see which sensors look faulty. If you want an extra step, drop those readings and run a plain least-squares fit on the rest.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers the model to **SNR ≈ 60.5 dB**, compared with about 2.5 dB from plain least squares.

**What I chose and why:** A few of the readings are 20–50 units off, while the normal noise is around 0.01. Ordinary least squares squares the residuals, so those few readings pull the whole answer off. I used **IRLS with an L1 data misfit** instead (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls(..., kind="data")`). It minimises the sum of absolute residuals `‖y − A x‖₁`, re-solving a weighted least-squares problem at each step and giving less weight to readings with large residuals. It doesn't need to know which readings are bad, so it only uses `y` and `A`, as in your real survey.

- **Settings:** 50 outer iterations, each one starting from the previous answer, with up to 200 LSQR iterations inside each. `epsR=1e-8` is a small damping term that stops the weights from blowing up when a residual is near zero. There's no extra regularisation on `x`, because with 300 observations and 50 unknowns the problem is already well-posed.
- **Outlier flags:** After the inversion, the script marks readings whose residual is more than 5 times a robust estimate of the typical residual (1.4826 × the median absolute deviation). This is something you can use on the real survey to see which sensors look faulty.

**Results:**
```
IRLS outer iterations: 50
Flagged 30 readings as outliers (|r| > 5*MAD, MAD=0.00972)
Flagged == injected outliers: True
Least-squares SNR (dB): 2.49
SNR_DB=60.4841
```
- The robust inversion improves on least squares by about 58 dB.
- The typical residual on the good readings is about 0.0097, which matches the 0.01 noise you added. So the remaining error comes from the noise itself, not from the outliers leaking through.
- All 30 flagged readings are exactly the ones your script corrupted. That check, and the SNR line, use `bad` and `xtrue`, which you won't have on real data.

On real data, if the faulty readings ever made up a large share of the total (roughly a third or more), L1 would start to struggle. In that case you could swap in a misfit that ignores large residuals even more strongly (such as Cauchy or Tukey), or re-invert using only the readings that weren't flagged.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers.

Least squares (L2 misfit) is pulled strongly by a few very large spikes in the
data. We instead minimise an L1 data misfit, ||y - A x||_1, with PyLops' data
IRLS solver: large residuals (outlier readings) are down-weighted at each
outer iteration. Only ``y`` and ``A`` are used for the inversion; ``xtrue`` is
used purely for evaluating the result.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- synthetic data (as provided) ---------------------------------------------
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# --- inversion: from here on only y and A are used -----------------------------
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# Baseline: plain least squares (for comparison only)
xls = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=200, atol=0, btol=0)[0]

# Robust: L1 data misfit via IRLS
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op,
    y,
    x0=np.zeros(m),
    nouter=50,
    epsR=1e-8,
    epsI=0.0,
    tolIRLS=1e-10,
    kind="data",
    warm=True,
    iter_lim=200,
)

# Flag suspicious readings from the robust residual (MAD-based threshold)
r = y - Op @ xinv
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))
flagged = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(r) > 5 * mad)

print(f"IRLS outer iterations: {nouter}")
print(f"Flagged {flagged.size} readings as outliers (|r| > 5*MAD, MAD={mad:.3g})")
print(f"Flagged == injected outliers: {set(flagged) == set(bad)}")
print(f"Least-squares SNR (dB): {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

import numpy as np

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The final line is **`SNR_DB=34.157`**, and the recovered model spans −144 to 299 kg/m³ against a true range of −150 to 300.

**What I chose and why**
- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion`, which runs PyLops' own LSQR. The regulariser is `pylops.SecondDerivative`, which pushes the model towards smoothness. It only uses PyLops operators and solvers, so moving to CuPy later should only mean switching the arrays.
- **Scaling:** with the 6.674e-11 factor the data are around 1e-7, which makes LSQR converge badly and makes ε meaningless. I divide both the operator and the data by `G·sum(h)`. Because both get the same factor, the model unknowns are still kg/m³ and nothing has to be converted back afterwards.
- **Choosing ε:** the noise σ is known (1% of max|d|), so I used the discrepancy principle. The script tries 41 values of ε from 1e-2 to 1e3 and keeps the largest one whose data residual is no bigger than √N·σ. This choice never looks at `xtrue`.

**How well it worked**
- **Chosen ε:** 5.62. LSQR stopped normally (`istop=2`) after 491 iterations.
- **Data fit:** the relative data residual is 2.35e-2, against an expected noise level of 2.42e-2. So it fits the data to the noise level without overfitting.
- **Model SNR:** 34.16 dB. For comparison, the best value on the ε grid was 35.65 dB at ε=4.2, but that was found using `xtrue`, which you won't have with real data. The principled choice gets within about 1.5 dB of it.
- Very small ε lets noise through into the model (SNR −0.7 dB at ε=0.01), and very large ε smooths away the anomalies (2.9 dB at ε=1000).

**Caveats**
- For the larger ε values the solver hit the 500-iteration cap, so the residuals printed for those rows may not have fully converged. The chosen ε is not affected, since it converged before the cap.
- The ε grid has about 0.125 decades between points, so a finer search between 4 and 8 could improve the result slightly.
- The script also saves `invert_result.npz`. It has no plots.

**Possible next steps:** use a finer ε search or an L-curve, or allow more iterations. For the GPU move, the operator, data and `x0` all need to be CuPy arrays.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (smooth density-contrast model).

Solver: pylops.regularized_inversion (LSQR on the stacked system
[Op; eps*D2] x = [d; 0]) with a second-derivative smoothness regulariser.
Everything is PyLops-native, so swapping numpy arrays for cupy ones later
should be enough to run it on GPU.

The operator carries G = 6.674e-11, so data are ~1e-7. To keep LSQR well
conditioned and eps dimensionless, operator and data are rescaled by the same
constant (the model stays in kg/m^3), then eps is picked with the discrepancy
principle using the known noise level.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11


def build_problem(rng):
    n = 200
    t = np.arange(-30, 31)
    h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
    Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=30, dtype="float64")

    z = np.arange(n)
    xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
        -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
    )
    d_clean = Op @ xtrue
    sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
    d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)
    return Op, d, xtrue, sigma, h


def invert(Ops, ys, D2, eps, niter):
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Ops, ys, [D2], epsRs=[eps], x0=np.zeros(Ops.shape[1]),
        iter_lim=niter, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12, show=False,
    )
    return xinv, istop, itn


def main(niter=500, seed=0):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    Op, d, xtrue, sigma, h = build_problem(rng)
    n = Op.shape[1]

    # adjoint check
    dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

    # rescale operator and data by the same constant: model stays in kg/m^3
    scale = G * h.sum()
    Ops = (1.0 / scale) * Op
    ys = d / scale
    sigmas = sigma / scale

    D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype="float64")

    # discrepancy principle: ||ys - Ops x|| ~ sqrt(N) * sigma
    target = np.sqrt(d.size) * sigmas
    epss = np.logspace(-2, 3, 41)
    results = []
    print(f"{'eps':>10} {'||r||/target':>13} {'iters':>6} {'SNR(dB)':>8}")
    for eps in epss:
        x, istop, itn = invert(Ops, ys, D2, eps, niter)
        ratio = np.linalg.norm(ys - Ops @ x) / target
        results.append((eps, ratio, x, istop, itn))
        print(f"{eps:10.3e} {ratio:13.3f} {itn:6d} {snr(xtrue, x):8.2f}")

    # largest eps whose residual does not exceed the noise level
    ok = [r for r in results if r[1] <= 1.0]
    eps, ratio, xinv, istop, itn = ok[-1] if ok else results[0]

    relres = np.linalg.norm(d - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(d)
    expected = sigma * np.sqrt(d.size) / np.linalg.norm(d)
    best = max(results, key=lambda r: snr(xtrue, r[2]))
    print()
    print(f"chosen eps (discrepancy principle) = {eps:.3e}")
    print(f"LSQR istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
    print(f"relative data residual = {relres:.3e} (noise level ~ {expected:.3e})")
    print(f"model range: [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}] kg/m^3 "
          f"(true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}])")
    print(f"oracle best on grid (uses xtrue, for reference only): "
          f"eps={best[0]:.3e}, SNR={snr(xtrue, best[2]):.2f} dB")

    np.savez("invert_result.npz", xinv=xinv, xtrue=xtrue, d=d, eps=eps)
    print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

- The ε grid has about 0.125 decades between points, so a finer search between 4 and 8 could improve the result slightly.
- The script also saves `invert_result.npz`. It has no plots.

**Possible next steps:** use a finer ε search or an L-curve, or allow more iterations. For the GPU move, the operator, data and `x0` all need to be CuPy arrays.

- Very small ε lets noise through into the model (SNR −0.7 dB at ε=0.01), and very large ε smooths away the anomalies (2.9 dB at ε=1000).

- For the larger ε values the solver hit the 500-iteration cap, so the residuals printed for those rows may not have fully converged. The chosen ε is not affected, since it converged before the cap.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward: d = G * (h * x), G = 6.674e-11, h an unnormalised Gaussian kernel.
Model:   density contrast x in kg/m^3 (expected smooth).

Solver: pylops.regularized_inversion with engine="pylops" (i.e. PyLops' cgls,
not scipy's lsqr) and a SecondDerivative smoothing regulariser. The weight
eps is chosen by the discrepancy principle (data residual ~ noise level),
which does not use the true model.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200
niter = 500

# ---- problem set-up ----
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

z = np.arange(nz)
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)
dclean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(dclean))
d = dclean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(nz)

dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# ---- scaling: data ~1e-7 and G ~1e-11 would stall cgls ----
# Normalise the operator by its largest singular value (power iteration, PyLops
# native) and the data by max|d|; solve for a dimensionless model xs and map
# back to kg/m^3 with x = xs * dscale / opnorm.
opnorm = np.sqrt(np.abs((Op.H @ Op).eigs(neigs=1, symmetric=True, niter=100)[0]))
dscale = np.max(np.abs(d))
Opn = (1.0 / opnorm) * Op
dn = d / dscale
sigman = sigma / dscale
mscale = dscale / opnorm

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, dtype="float64")
target = sigman * np.sqrt(nz)  # expected ||noise|| in scaled units


def invert(eps):
    xs, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Opn, dn, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=niter, tol=1e-12
    )
    return xs, istop, itn, np.linalg.norm(dn - Opn @ xs)


# ---- discrepancy principle sweep over eps (to refine: tune-regularization) ----
epss = np.logspace(-3, 2, 26)
results = [invert(e) for e in epss]
resnorms = np.array([r[3] for r in results])
ibest = np.argmin(np.abs(np.log(resnorms / target)))
eps = epss[ibest]
xs, istop, itn, resn = results[ibest]
xinv = xs * mscale  # back to kg/m^3

print(f"Op.shape={Op.shape}, opnorm={opnorm:.3e}, max|d|={dscale:.3e}, sigma={sigma:.3e}")
print("eps sweep (eps, ||r||/||noise||):")
for e, r in zip(epss, resnorms):
    print(f"  {e:9.3e}  {r / target:7.3f}{'  <-- chosen' if e == eps else ''}")
print(f"solver=cgls via regularized_inversion(engine='pylops'), eps={eps:.3e}, "
      f"istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
print(f"relative data residual = {np.linalg.norm(d - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e} "
      f"(expected from noise ~ {sigma * np.sqrt(nz) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e})")
print(f"model range: true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}], "
      f"inverted [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}] kg/m^3")

np.savez("invert_result.npz", z=z, xtrue=xtrue, xinv=xinv, d=d, eps=eps)

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` penalty. This uses PyLops' own LSQR on the augmented system, so it carries over to CuPy. The second-derivative penalty suits a smooth density model. Without it, the wide Gaussian kernel would let noise blow up in the high frequencies.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward: d = G * (h * x), G = 6.674e-11, h an unnormalised Gaussian kernel.
Model: density contrast in kg/m^3 (kept in these units throughout).

Solver: pylops.regularized_inversion (LSQR on the augmented system) with a
SecondDerivative smoothing regulariser. epsR is chosen by the discrepancy
principle (data residual ~ known noise norm), not from the true model.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- problem
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)
dclean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(dclean))
d = dclean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(nz)

dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- scaling
# Multiply operator and data by the same factor 1/G: the solution x (kg/m^3)
# is unchanged, but the system becomes O(1)-O(10) and epsR has a sane scale.
s = 1.0 / G
Ops = s * Op
ds = s * d
sigmas = s * sigma
target = sigmas * np.sqrt(nz)  # expected ||noise|| in scaled units

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, dtype="float64")


def invert(eps, niter=500):
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Ops, ds, [D2], epsRs=[eps], x0=np.zeros(nz), iter_lim=niter,
        atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12, show=False,
    )
    return xinv, istop, itn


# ---------------------------------------------- discrepancy-principle sweep
epss = np.logspace(-2, 3, 41)
results = []
for eps in epss:
    xinv, istop, itn = invert(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(ds - Ops @ xinv)
    results.append((eps, res, xinv, istop, itn))

# largest eps whose residual does not exceed the noise level
ok = [r for r in results if r[1] <= target]
eps, res, xinv, istop, itn = ok[-1] if ok else results[0]

print(f"Op.shape={Op.shape}, sigma={sigma:.3e} (1% of max|d|={np.max(np.abs(dclean)):.3e})")
print("eps sweep (eps, ||r||/||noise||):")
for e, r, *_ in results[::4]:
    print(f"  eps={e:9.3e}  ratio={r / target:6.3f}")
print(f"chosen epsR={eps:.3e}, istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
print(f"relative data residual = {np.linalg.norm(d - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e}")
print(f"expected noise rel. res = {sigma * np.sqrt(nz) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e}")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I saved `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It finishes in about 1 s and ends with:

```
SNR_DB=34.96965243721508
```

**What I chose**

1. **Scaling.** Because of G = 6.674e-11, the data are around 1e-7 while the model is around 1e2. In SI units the regularisation weight would have to be about 1e-11, which is hard to tune and numerically fragile. Since G is a known constant, I solve the equivalent problem `(Op/G) x = d/G` instead. The model `x` is still in kg/m³, so no conversion is needed afterwards; only the operator and data are rescaled.
2. **Regularisation.** Because the model should be smooth, I penalise its second derivative (`pylops.SecondDerivative`, with `edge=True`).
3. **Solver.** I used `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own CGLS rather than scipy's. It should therefore move to CuPy without changes.
4. **Choosing the weight.** I picked it with the discrepancy principle: I scanned 41 values from 1e-2 to 1e3 and kept the largest one whose data misfit stays within the expected noise level (√N·σ, with σ known from the 1% noise spec). This doesn't use the true model. It chose **eps = 100**, with a scaled misfit of 720 against a target of 751.

**How well it worked**

- **Model SNR:** about **35.0 dB** against the true model.
- **Amplitudes:** the result ranges from −145 to 299 kg/m³, close to the true −150 to 300.
- **Weight choice:** I ran a separate check against the true model to see how sensitive the result is to the weight. Its SNR was 24.7, 30.2, **35.0**, 25.1 and 16.8 dB at weights of 10, 30, **100**, 300 and 1000. So the noise-based rule landed on the best of the weights I compared. This check is not part of the script.

The scan runs 42 solves, which is cheap at this size. On a much larger CuPy problem you might narrow the range of weights or replace the scan with a few bisection steps.

"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Strategy
--------
* The physical operator carries G = 6.674e-11, so data are ~1e-7 while the
  model is ~1e2 kg/m^3. Regularisation weights would then have to be ~1e-11,
  which is awkward and numerically fragile. Since G is a known scalar, we
  invert the equivalent problem  (Op / G) x = d / G : the model x stays in
  kg/m^3, only the data/operator are rescaled.
* Smooth model -> Tikhonov regularisation with a second-derivative operator,
  solved with PyLops' own CGLS (regularized_inversion, engine="pylops").
* The regularisation weight is chosen with the discrepancy principle
  (residual norm ~ sqrt(N) * sigma, sigma known from the noise level),
  so the choice does not use the true model.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200

# ---------------- forward problem ----------------
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8.0) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

z = np.arange(nz)
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
d = d_clean + rng.normal(0.0, sigma, nz)

# ---------------- rescaled problem ----------------
Opn = (1.0 / G) * Op  # unit-scale operator, model still in kg/m^3
dn = d / G
sigman = sigma / G
target = np.sqrt(nz) * sigman  # expected residual norm

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True, dtype="float64")


def solve(eps):
    x = regularized_inversion(
        Opn, dn, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=500, tol=1e-12
    )[0]
    return x, np.linalg.norm(Opn @ x - dn)


# ---------------- discrepancy principle: scan eps ----------------
epss = np.logspace(-2, 3, 41)
res = []
for eps in epss:
    _, r = solve(eps)
    res.append(r)
res = np.array(res)
# residual grows with eps: take the largest eps whose residual <= target
ok = np.where(res <= target)[0]
eps_best = epss[ok[-1]] if ok.size else epss[0]
xinv, rbest = solve(eps_best)

print(f"noise sigma (SI)          = {sigma:.3e}")
print(f"target residual (scaled)  = {target:.3e}")
print(f"chosen eps (D2 weight)    = {eps_best:.3e}")
print(f"achieved residual (scaled)= {rbest:.3e}")
print(f"model range [kg/m^3]      = {xinv.min():.1f} .. {xinv.max():.1f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

**What I chose**

1. **Scaling.** Because of G = 6.674e-11, the data are around 1e-7 while the model is around 1e2. In SI units the regularisation weight would have to be about 1e-11, which is hard to tune and numerically fragile. Since G is a known constant, I solve the equivalent problem `(Op/G) x = d/G` instead. The model `x` is still in kg/m³, so no conversion is needed afterwards; only the operator and data are rescaled.
2. **Regularisation.** Because the model should be smooth, I penalise its second derivative (`pylops.SecondDerivative`, with `edge=True`).
3. **Solver.** I used `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own CGLS rather than scipy's. It should therefore move to CuPy without changes.
4. **Choosing the weight.** I picked it with the discrepancy principle: I scanned 41 values from 1e-2 to 1e3 and kept the largest one whose data misfit stays within the expected noise level (√N·σ, with σ known from the 1% noise spec). This doesn't use the true model. It chose **eps = 100**, with a scaled misfit of 720 against a target of 751.

**How well it worked**

- **Model SNR:** about **35.0 dB** against the true model.
- **Amplitudes:** the result ranges from −145 to 299 kg/m³, close to the true −150 to 300.
- **Weight choice:** I ran a separate check against the true model to see how sensitive the result is to the weight. Its SNR was 24.7, 30.2, **35.0**, 25.1 and 16.8 dB at weights of 10, 30, **100**, 300 and 1000. So the noise-based rule landed on the best of the weights I compared. This check is not part of the script.

The scan runs 42 solves, which is cheap at this size. On a much larger CuPy problem you might narrow the range of weights or replace the scan with a few bisection steps.

I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and it finishes with **`SNR_DB=34.969652437225164`**. The inverted model ranges from −145.0 to 299.2 kg/m³, against −149.9 to 300.0 for the true model, so both anomalies come back with the right shape and close to the right amplitude.

**What I chose**

- **Scaling G out of the inversion.** With G = 6.674e-11 left in the operator, the data are around 1e-8. That makes the regularisation weight and solver tolerance land at awkward values like 1e-11. So I divide the data by G and invert with the plain `Convolve1D`. This only changes the units of the data, not the model: `xinv` stays in kg/m³, and the script checks the fit against the original `Op` in SI units (misfit 4.80e-8 against an expected noise norm of 5.01e-8).
- **Smoothness.** I use a second-derivative (Tikhonov) penalty: minimise ‖d/G − Conv·x‖² + ε²‖D₂x‖², with `pylops.SecondDerivative(edge=True)`.
- **Solver.** `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own CGLS. Nothing comes from scipy, so it should move to CuPy by passing CuPy arrays.
- **Choosing ε.** I scan ε over 1e-2 to 1e3 and use the discrepancy principle: the largest ε whose data misfit is still within the known noise level, √N·σ. This uses only the noise level, not the true model. It picks **ε = 100**, with misfit at 0.96 of the noise level.

**How well it worked**

- The scan also prints the SNR for every ε. The best possible value in the scan, which you could only find by knowing the true model, is 35.8 dB at ε ≈ 75. The automatic choice gets 35.0 dB, within about 1 dB of that.
- With weak regularisation (ε < 0.2) the noise gets amplified and the SNR is negative, so the smoothness penalty is doing real work here.
- A remaining limitation: the noise level is taken as known, because it's 1% of max|d| by construction. With real data you'd need an estimate of σ, or a different way to pick ε such as the L-curve or generalised cross-validation.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward:  d = G * conv(h, x) + noise,  x = density contrast [kg/m^3]
Inverse:  min_x ||d/G - C x||^2 + eps^2 ||D2 x||^2   (smoothness via 2nd derivative)

The operator is applied with G = 6.674e-11 folded out: the data are divided by G
and the inversion uses the unit-scaled convolution. This is a pure change of units
of the data (the model stays in kg/m^3), and it keeps the regularisation weight
and solver tolerances in a sensible numeric range instead of ~1e-11.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)

# Operator
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Conv = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)
Op = G * Conv

# True model [kg/m^3] and data [SI]
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)
d_clean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
d = d_clean + rng.normal(0, sigma, nz)

# Rescale data to "G-free" units; inversion uses the unit-scaled operator
dn = d / G
sigman = sigma / G

# Smoothness regularisation: second derivative
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True)


def invert(eps):
    x = regularized_inversion(
        Conv, dn, [D2], epsRs=[eps], x0=np.zeros(nz),
        engine="pylops", niter=500, tol=1e-12,
    )[0]
    return x


# Choose eps with the discrepancy principle: ||dn - Conv x|| ~= sqrt(N) * sigma
# (uses only the known noise level, not the true model)
target = np.sqrt(nz) * sigman
epss = np.logspace(-2, 3, 41)
results = []
for eps in epss:
    x = invert(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(dn - Conv @ x)
    results.append((eps, x, res))
    print(f"eps={eps:9.3e}  residual/target={res / target:6.3f}  "
          f"SNR={snr(xtrue, x):6.2f} dB")

# Largest eps whose residual does not exceed the target (smoothest acceptable model)
ok = [r for r in results if r[2] <= target]
eps_best, xinv, res_best = ok[-1] if ok else results[0]

print(f"\nChosen eps (discrepancy principle) = {eps_best:.3e}, "
      f"residual/target = {res_best / target:.3f}")
print(f"Data misfit (SI units): {np.linalg.norm(d - Op @ xinv):.3e} "
      f"vs noise norm ~ {np.sqrt(nz) * sigma:.3e}")
print(f"Max |xinv| = {np.max(np.abs(xinv)):.1f} kg/m^3, "
      f"xinv range [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}] kg/m^3 "
      f"(true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}])")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
