Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward: d = G * (h * x), G = 6.674e-11, h = unnormalised Gaussian (sigma = 8 samples).
Model x is a density contrast in kg/m^3 and is kept in those units throughout.

Strategy
--------
* The raw operator has a norm of ~1e-9, so any damping/regularisation weight would be
  meaningless in absolute terms. We divide operator AND data by the operator's largest
  singular value (estimated with PyLops' own power iteration). This leaves the solution
  (and its units) unchanged but makes the operator unit-norm, so the regularisation
  weight is O(1) and CGLS is well conditioned.
* Smoothness prior: Tikhonov regularisation with a second-derivative operator.
* Solver: pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion with engine="pylops"
  (CGLS), which is backend-agnostic and therefore works with CuPy arrays.
* Regularisation weight: discrepancy principle -- pick the epsilon whose data residual
  matches the expected noise norm sqrt(N) * sigma_noise (no use of the true model).
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)

# Operator
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

# True model (kg/m^3) and data
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)
d_clean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma_n = 0.01 * np.abs(d_clean).max()
d = d_clean + rng.normal(0, sigma_n, nz)

# Rescale operator and data by the operator's largest singular value
opnorm = np.sqrt(np.abs((Op.H @ Op).eigs(neigs=1, symmetric=True, niter=100)[0]))
Ops = (1.0 / opnorm) * Op
ds = d / opnorm
target = np.sqrt(nz) * sigma_n / opnorm  # expected noise norm in scaled data

# Smoothness regularisation
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True, dtype="float64")


def solve(eps):
    return regularized_inversion(
        Ops, ds, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=1000, tol=1e-12
    )[0]


# Discrepancy principle on a log grid of epsilons
epss = np.logspace(-4, 1, 41)
res = []
for eps in epss:
    x = solve(eps)
    res.append(np.linalg.norm(Ops @ x - ds))
res = np.array(res)
# residual grows with eps; choose the largest eps whose residual does not exceed target
ok = np.where(res <= target)[0]
ibest = ok[-1] if len(ok) else 0
eps_best = epss[ibest]
xinv = solve(eps_best)

print(f"operator norm (SI)      : {opnorm:.3e}")
print(f"noise std (data units)  : {sigma_n:.3e}")
print(f"chosen epsR (scaled)    : {eps_best:.3e}")
print(f"residual / noise target : {res[ibest] / target:.3f}")
print(f"model range kg/m^3      : true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}], "
      f"inv [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}]")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it in about 1.3 s. The inverted model has an **SNR of 34.2 dB** against the true model. The script's last line is `SNR_DB=34.212115952921096`.

```
operator norm (SI)      : 1.329e-09
noise std (data units)  : 3.543e-09
chosen epsR (scaled)    : 5.623e+00
residual / noise target : 0.973
model range kg/m^3      : true [-149.9, 300.0], inv [-144.1, 298.8]
SNR_DB=34.212115952921096
```

**What I chose:**

1. **Rescaling for the SI units.** Because of the `6.674e-11` factor, the operator's norm is about 1.3e-9. At that scale, any regularisation weight means nothing. So I divide both the operator and the data by the operator's largest singular value, which I estimate with PyLops' own `eigs`. This gives the same solution in the same units (kg/m³), and the regularisation weight becomes a normal O(1) number.
2. **Smoothness prior.** I used Tikhonov regularisation with `pylops.SecondDerivative` (`edge=True`) as the regulariser.
3. **Solver.** I used `regularized_inversion(..., engine="pylops")`, which runs PyLops' own CGLS rather than scipy's `lsqr`. It is backend-agnostic, so it should work once you switch to CuPy.
4. **Choosing the weight.** I tried 41 log-spaced values of ε and kept the largest one whose data misfit is no bigger than the expected noise norm, √N·σ. This uses only the known noise level, not the true model. The chosen value is ε ≈ 5.6, which gives a misfit of 0.97× the noise level.

**How well it worked:** Both anomalies come back in the right places with nearly the right amplitudes. The positive peak is 298.8 against a true 300, and the negative one is −144.1 against −150. The small loss of amplitude in the narrower negative body is the usual smoothing bias from the regulariser.

**Before moving to CuPy:** most of the script is already backend-agnostic. The data and noise setup and `np.linalg.norm` in the ε search still use NumPy directly. Swap those for `cupy` or `pylops.utils.backend.get_module`.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers the signal almost exactly: **SNR_DB=65.05**.

**What I chose and why**
- **Sparse in the DCT:** each of your cosines, `cos(πf(2t+1)/(2n))`, is exactly one basis function of the DCT-II (a standard cosine transform). So the signal has only 12 non-zero coefficients out of 512. I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT`, which is orthonormal by default, and solved for the coefficients `c` in `y = R·Dᴴ·c`, then rebuilt `x = Dᴴ·c`. The operator passes the dot-test, a check that its forward and adjoint are consistent.
- **Solver: FISTA** (L1 sparsity, 500 iterations, `eps = σ·√(2 ln n) ≈ 0.018`). With a known noise level of σ = 0.005, this standard threshold rule gives a reasonable starting value. I didn't use spgl1 because it isn't installed.
- **Debiasing:** an L1 solver always shrinks amplitudes slightly. So I kept the coefficients FISTA found, refit just those with `lsqr`, and then rebuilt the signal.

**Results**
| | |
|---|---|
| Coefficients found | 12, all 12 true ones and no false ones |
| SNR straight from FISTA | 51.8 dB |
| SNR after debiasing | **65.0 dB** |
| Relative data residual | 1.48e-3 vs. noise level ≈ 1.60e-3 |

The residual is about at the noise level, so the result fits the data without fitting the noise.

**Notes**
- FISTA used all 500 iterations without hitting `tol`. That didn't matter here because the coefficients were already clearly identified, but on harder data you may need more iterations.
- The script also saves the reconstruction to `xinv.npy`.
- This works so well because your test signal sits exactly on the DCT grid. A real "periodic-looking" signal whose frequencies fall between grid points won't be exactly sparse. In that case, expect a lower SNR. You'd then need to tune `eps` and the `10·eps` cutoff that decides which coefficients to keep, or try a different transform. I can help with either if your real data falls short.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Compressive-sensing reconstruction of a 1/4-sampled signal with PyLops.

The signal is a sum of cosines on the DCT-II grid, so it is sparse in the
orthonormal DCT. We solve the synthesis problem

    min_c 0.5 ||y - R D^H c||_2^2 + eps ||c||_1,   x = D^H c

with FISTA, then debias with a least-squares fit on the recovered support.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- data
rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)
sigma = 0.005

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- operators
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)  # orthonormal, signal -> coeffs
Op = R @ D.H  # coeffs -> recorded samples
pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape, atol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- FISTA
# Threshold ~ noise level of the back-projected data (universal threshold);
# a starting value, to be tuned (see tune-regularization).
eps = sigma * np.sqrt(2 * np.log(n))
cinv, niter_used, cost = fista(Op, y, niter=500, eps=eps, tol=1e-10, show=False)
print(f"FISTA: eps={eps:.3g}, iterations={niter_used}, "
      f"cost {cost[0]:.3e} -> {cost[-1]:.3e}")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- debias
# L1 shrinks amplitudes; refit the selected coefficients by least squares.
support = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(cinv) > 10 * eps)
print(f"support size: {support.size}  "
      f"(true DCT indices recovered: {np.isin(np.sort(freqs), support).sum()}/12)")
S = pylops.Restriction(n, support)
cs = lsqr(Op @ S.H, y, x0=np.zeros(support.size), niter=100, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)[0]
cdeb = S.H @ cs
xinv = D.H @ cdeb

res = np.linalg.norm(y - R @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(y)
print(f"relative data residual: {res:.2e} (noise level ~ "
      f"{sigma * np.sqrt(len(y)) / np.linalg.norm(y):.2e})")
print(f"SNR without debiasing: {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, D.H @ cinv):.2f} dB")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)

print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote and ran `invert.py`. The reconstruction recovered all 12 frequencies exactly and reached **SNR = 65.0 dB** against `xtrue`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Sparsifying basis:** your signal is a sum of cosines of the form `cos(π f (2t+1)/(2n))`, which are exactly the DCT-II basis functions. So in the orthonormal `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` it has only 12 non-zero values out of 512.
- **Problem type:** 128 samples for 512 unknowns is underdetermined, so I set it up as compressive sensing. The operator `Op = R @ D.H` maps sparse DCT coefficients `p` to your recorded samples. It passes the dot-test, which checks that its adjoint is correct.
- **Solver:** FISTA (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista`) minimises `½‖y − Op p‖² + eps‖p‖₁`, with `eps` set to 1% of `max|Op.H y|` (≈0.099). The script then builds `xinv = D.H @ p`. I didn't use `spgl1` because it isn't installed.
- **Debiasing:** the L1 penalty shrinks the recovered amplitudes a little. After FISTA, the script refits the coefficients by plain least squares using only the 12 frequencies it found. This took the SNR from 38 dB to 65 dB.

- SNR is 38.1 dB from FISTA alone and **65.0 dB** after the refit. The last line printed is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203583`.
- The script also saves the reconstruction to `xinv.npy`.

**Caveats**
- `eps` and the 1e-3 cutoff for which coefficients count as "found" are set by rule of thumb, not tuned.
- The refit only helps if the right frequencies are found. On real data the signal may not be exactly sparse in the DCT. If that happens, check that the misfit stays near your noise level, and consider tuning `eps` (the `tune-regularization` skill can do that) or using overlapping windowed DCTs.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Compressive-sensing reconstruction of a DCT-sparse signal from 1/4 of its samples.

Model: y = R x + n, with x = D^H p and p sparse (D = orthonormal DCT-II).
Solve  min_p 0.5 ||y - R D^H p||_2^2 + eps ||p||_1  with FISTA (synthesis form),
then reconstruct xinv = D^H p.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic setup (as provided) ----
rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# ---- operators ----
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)  # orthonormal: D^H D = I
Op = R @ D.H                                     # sparse DCT coeffs -> recorded samples
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6)

# ---- sparse inversion ----
# eps: L1 weight, set as a small fraction of max|Op^H y| (to be tuned)
eps = 1e-2 * np.abs(Op.H @ y).max()
niter = 500
pinv, niters, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(
    Op, y, niter=niter, eps=eps, tol=1e-10, show=False
)

# Debias: least-squares refit on the detected support (removes L1 shrinkage)
support = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(pinv) > 1e-3 * np.abs(pinv).max())
A = Op.todense()[:, support]
pdeb = np.zeros(n)
pdeb[support] = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]

xinv_fista = D.H @ pinv
xinv = D.H @ pdeb

# ---- diagnostics ----
noise_rel = 0.005 * np.sqrt(128) / np.linalg.norm(y)
res_rel = np.linalg.norm(y - R @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(y)
true_support = np.sort(freqs)
print(f"FISTA: eps={eps:.3g}, iterations={niters}, cost {cost[0]:.3g} -> {cost[-1]:.3g}")
print(f"support found ({support.size}): {support.tolist()}")
print(f"true freqs     ({true_support.size}): {true_support.tolist()}")
print(f"relative data residual: {res_rel:.2e} (expected noise level ~{noise_rel:.2e})")
print(f"SNR FISTA only: {snr(xtrue, xinv_fista):.2f} dB")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The reconstruction recovered the signal almost perfectly: **SNR = 65.0 dB**. `invert.py` ran successfully and prints `SNR_DB=65.0468` as its last line.

**What I chose**
- **Sparsity in the DCT domain.** Your cosines `cos(πf(2t+1)/2n)` are exactly the DCT-II basis functions, so the true signal has only 12 non-zero coefficients under `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT`. PyLops 2.8's DCT is orthonormal by default, so I didn't pass a normalisation argument.
- **Synthesis-form problem solved with FISTA.** The script finds DCT coefficients `p` that fit the recorded samples (operator `R @ D.H`) while keeping as few coefficients as possible. Then it builds the signal as `xinv = D.H @ p`. I used FISTA because `spgl1` isn't installed. The operator passes PyLops' adjoint check (dot-test).
- **Threshold.** I set it from the noise level, `eps = σ·√(2 ln n) ≈ 0.018`. This is a reasonable starting value, not a tuned one.
- **Debiasing step.** The sparsity penalty pulls the recovered amplitudes slightly towards zero. To undo that, the script refits the amplitudes by least squares using only the coefficients FISTA kept.

**How well it worked**
- **Frequencies:** FISTA found all 12 true frequencies and no false ones.
- **Data fit:** the relative misfit to the recorded samples is 1.48e-3, just under the noise level of about 1.60e-3, so it matches the data without fitting the noise.
- **Accuracy:** FISTA alone gives 51.8 dB, and the debiasing step raises it to **65.0 dB**.
- **Iterations:** FISTA used all 400 allowed iterations; it didn't stop early on its tolerance. The answer is already accurate, but you can lower `niter` or loosen `tol` if speed matters.

The script also saves the reconstruction to `xinv.npy`.

**Caveat for real data:** this works so well because the synthetic signal is exactly sparse in the DCT basis. If your real signal's frequencies don't land exactly on DCT bins, the energy spreads across neighbouring coefficients. In that case, raise `eps` slightly or try other transforms (the `pick-sparsifying-transform` skill can compare them). Use `tune-regularization` to set `eps` properly.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Reconstruct a 512-sample signal from 128 random samples via sparsity in the DCT domain.

The signal is a sum of DCT-II basis functions, so it is sparse under an
orthonormal DCT. We solve the synthesis problem

    min_p 0.5 ||y - R D^H p||_2^2 + eps ||p||_1

with FISTA and reconstruct xinv = D^H p.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- data (user setup)
rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
sigma = 0.005
y = R @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(128)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- operators
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)  # signal -> coefficients
Op = R @ D.H                                                   # coefficients -> data
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- sparse inversion
# Threshold on the order of the noise level per coefficient (universal threshold)
eps = sigma * np.sqrt(2 * np.log(n))  # to be tuned
niter = 400
pinv, niters, cost = fista(Op, y, niter=niter, eps=eps, tol=1e-10, show=False)

# Debias: least-squares refit on the recovered support (removes L1 shrinkage)
support = np.where(np.abs(pinv) > 1e-3 * np.abs(pinv).max())[0]
A = Op.todense()[:, support]
pinv_db = np.zeros_like(pinv)
pinv_db[support] = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]

xinv_fista = D.H @ pinv
xinv = D.H @ pinv_db

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- diagnostics
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
res = np.linalg.norm(y - R @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(y)
noise_rel = sigma * np.sqrt(len(y)) / np.linalg.norm(y)
true_support = np.sort(freqs)
print(f"FISTA: iterations={niters}, cost {cost[0]:.3e} -> {cost[-1]:.3e}, eps={eps:.4f}")
print(f"Recovered support ({len(support)}): {support.tolist()}")
print(f"True frequencies  ({len(true_support)}): {true_support.tolist()}")
print(f"Relative data residual {res:.2e} (noise level ~{noise_rel:.2e})")
print(f"SNR FISTA only     = {snr(xtrue, xinv_fista):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The reconstruction recovered the full signal with **SNR ≈ 65 dB**. `invert.py` runs and its last line is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203583`.

**What I chose**
- **Sparse basis:** each part of your signal, `cos(π f (2t+1) / 2n)`, is exactly a DCT-II basis function. So the whole signal has only 12 non-zero coefficients in `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` (orthonormal, type 2). That makes this a compressive-sensing problem: 128 measurements are plenty for a signal with 12 non-zero coefficients out of 512.
- **Solver:** FISTA (sparse L1-regularised recovery) on `R @ DCT.H`, solving `min ||y − R DCTᴴ c||² + ε||c||₁` with ε = 1e-2 and 2000 iterations.
- **Final cleanup:** L1 regularisation slightly shrinks the amplitudes. So I kept the coefficients FISTA found (anything above 1% of the largest) and refit their values with ordinary least squares on the measured samples. Then `xinv = DCTᴴ c`.

**How well it worked**
- FISTA found exactly 12 non-zero coefficients, at exactly the true frequencies.
- SNR without the final refit: about 54 dB. With it: about 65 dB. What error is left comes from the 0.005 measurement noise.

One caveat: this works so well because the frequencies in this synthetic example fall exactly on the DCT grid. If your real signal's frequencies fall between grid points, its coefficients will spread out (spectral leakage) and you should expect a lower SNR. In that case, set the support cut-off and ε against your actual noise level.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The signal is a sum of DCT-II basis functions -> sparse in the DCT domain.
# Solve min ||y - R DCT^H c||_2^2 + eps ||c||_1, then x = DCT^H c.
DCTop = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)  # orthonormal
Op = R @ DCTop.H

cinv = fista(Op, y, niter=2000, eps=1e-2, tol=1e-10, show=False)[0]

# Debias: least-squares refit on the detected support
support = np.abs(cinv) > 1e-2 * np.abs(cinv).max()
A = Op.todense()[:, support]
cdeb = np.zeros(n)
cdeb[support] = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]

xinv = DCTop.H @ cdeb

print(f"Non-zero DCT coefficients recovered: {support.sum()} (true: {len(freqs)})")
print(f"Support matches true frequencies: {set(np.where(support)[0]) == set(freqs)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

The reconstruction recovered the full signal with **SNR ≈ 65 dB**. `invert.py` runs and its last line is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203583`.

- **Sparse basis:** each part of your signal, `cos(π f (2t+1) / 2n)`, is exactly a DCT-II basis function. So the whole signal has only 12 non-zero coefficients in `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` (orthonormal, type 2). That makes this a compressive-sensing problem: 128 measurements are plenty for a signal with 12 non-zero coefficients out of 512.
- **Solver:** FISTA (sparse L1-regularised recovery) on `R @ DCT.H`, solving `min ||y − R DCTᴴ c||² + ε||c||₁` with ε = 1e-2 and 2000 iterations.
- **Final cleanup:** L1 regularisation slightly shrinks the amplitudes. So I kept the coefficients FISTA found (anything above 1% of the largest) and refit their values with ordinary least squares on the measured samples. Then `xinv = DCTᴴ c`.

**How well it worked**

In that case, set the support cut-off and ε against your actual noise level.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The reconstruction is close to perfect: **SNR_DB=65.05**.

**What I chose**
- **Sparse basis:** each term in your signal, `cos(π f (2t+1) / 2n)`, is exactly one DCT-II basis function. So the signal is 12-sparse in the DCT domain, and I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` (orthonormal). This turns the task into a standard compressive-sensing problem: find sparse coefficients `c` such that `y ≈ R · Dᴴ · c`, then set `xinv = Dᴴ c`.
- **Solver:** FISTA (L1-regularised, soft thresholding), with `eps=0.05` and up to 2000 iterations. It converged in 372.
- **Debiasing:** the L1 penalty shrinks the amplitudes slightly. To undo that, I kept the coefficients above 5% of the largest one and re-fitted just those with least squares.

**How well it worked**
- FISTA found exactly 12 nonzero DCT coefficients, and they are the 12 true frequencies, with no spurious ones.
- SNR with FISTA alone: 43.8 dB.
- SNR after debiasing: **65.05 dB**, recovering all 512 samples from 128.

**Caveats for real data**
- This setup is ideal: the signal is exactly sparse in the DCT. Real recordings won't be exactly sparse, so expect lower SNR. Mainly, there'll be more small coefficients that make the support threshold harder to pick.
- Scale `eps` with your noise level. I picked 0.05 by hand for noise with standard deviation 0.005. If the noise is much higher, increase it.
- If debiasing picks up false coefficients on real data, use the FISTA-only result (`D.H @ c_l1`), which doesn't depend on the threshold.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- data (user setup) ---
rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# --- inversion: sparsity in the (orthonormal) DCT-II domain ---
# The signal is a sum of DCT-II atoms, so x = D^H c with c sparse.
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)
Op = R @ D.H

# 1) L1-regularised inversion (FISTA) to find the sparse support
eps = 0.05
c_l1, niter, cost = fista(Op, y, niter=2000, eps=eps, tol=1e-10)

# 2) Debias: least-squares refit of the coefficients on the detected support
#    (removes the amplitude shrinkage introduced by the soft threshold)
thresh = 0.05 * np.abs(c_l1).max()
support = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(c_l1) > thresh)
S = pylops.Restriction(n, support)
c_sup = np.linalg.lstsq((Op @ S.H).todense(), y, rcond=None)[0]
c = S.H @ c_sup

xinv = D.H @ c

print(f"FISTA iterations: {niter}")
print(f"Support size: {len(support)} (true: {len(freqs)})")
print(f"True DCT indices recovered: {set(freqs) <= set(support)}")
print(f"SNR (L1 only, before debias): {snr(xtrue, D.H @ c_l1):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The reconstruction is close to perfect: **SNR_DB=65.05**.

**What I chose**
- **Sparse basis:** each term in your signal, `cos(π f (2t+1) / 2n)`, is exactly one DCT-II basis function. So the signal is 12-sparse in the DCT domain, and I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` (orthonormal). This turns the task into a standard compressive-sensing problem: find sparse coefficients `c` such that `y ≈ R · Dᴴ · c`, then set `xinv = Dᴴ c`.
- **Solver:** FISTA (L1-regularised, soft thresholding), with `eps=0.05` and up to 2000 iterations. It converged in 372.
- **Debiasing:** the L1 penalty shrinks the amplitudes slightly. To undo that, I kept the coefficients above 5% of the largest one and re-fitted just those with least squares.

**How well it worked**
- FISTA found exactly 12 nonzero DCT coefficients, and they are the 12 true frequencies, with no spurious ones.
- SNR with FISTA alone: 43.8 dB.
- SNR after debiasing: **65.05 dB**, recovering all 512 samples from 128.

**Caveats for real data**
- This setup is ideal: the signal is exactly sparse in the DCT. Real recordings won't be exactly sparse, so expect lower SNR. Mainly, there'll be more small coefficients that make the support threshold harder to pick.
- Scale `eps` with your noise level. I picked 0.05 by hand for noise with standard deviation 0.005. If the noise is much higher, increase it.
- If debiasing picks up false coefficients on real data, use the FISTA-only result (`D.H @ c_l1`), which doesn't depend on the threshold.

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote and ran `invert.py`. The reconstruction recovers the signal almost exactly: **SNR = 65.0 dB**, from 128 of the 512 samples.

**What I chose**
- **Basis:** Each term `cos(πf(2t+1)/(2n))` in your signal is exactly one basis function of the DCT-II (a type of discrete cosine transform). So the full signal has only 12 non-zero DCT coefficients out of 512. I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT`, which is orthonormal, so its adjoint (`D.H`) is its inverse.
- **Problem:** I solve `y = R · Dᴴ · p` for the coefficients `p`, assuming most of them are zero. The full signal is then `xinv = Dᴴ p`. Plain least squares can't do this because 128 equations for 512 unknowns has infinitely many solutions. Assuming `p` is sparse is what lets it work: the cosine basis spreads each component across all samples, while the recorded samples are randomly placed.
- **Solver:** I used `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista` (an L1-regularised solver), with `eps=1e-2` and 500 iterations.
- **Debiasing:** L1 solvers shrink the amplitudes they recover. So I kept the coefficients above 5% of the largest one and refit their amplitudes with ordinary least squares.

**How well it worked**
| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| FISTA alone | 54.3 dB |
| FISTA + debias (final `xinv`) | **65.0 dB** |

FISTA found exactly the 12 true frequencies (15, 48, 60, 62, 113, 120, 132, 151, 163, 172, 175, 178), with no false ones. The last printed line is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203583`, computed with `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)` on the reconstructed full signal.

**For your real data**
- This works so well because the synthetic cosines line up exactly with the DCT grid. Real periodic signals usually sit between grid frequencies, so their energy spreads over several coefficients. Expect a lower SNR, and you may need to tune `eps` or the debias threshold.
- If the 5% threshold gives a poor result on your real data, use the FISTA-only result instead, which is still good.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The signal is a sum of DCT-II atoms -> sparse in the (orthonormal) DCT domain.
# Model x = DCT^H p, data y = R DCT^H p, solve for sparse p with FISTA (L1).
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(dims=n, type=2)
Op = R @ D.H

pinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(
    Op, y, niter=500, eps=1e-2, tol=1e-8, show=False
)[0]

# Debias: least-squares refit on the detected support
support = np.where(np.abs(pinv) > 0.05 * np.abs(pinv).max())[0]
Asup = Op.todense()[:, support]
pdeb = np.zeros(n)
pdeb[support] = np.linalg.lstsq(Asup, y, rcond=None)[0]

xinv_fista = D.H @ pinv
xinv = D.H @ pdeb

print(f"Detected DCT support: {len(support)} coefficients (true: {len(freqs)})")
print(f"True freqs:     {np.sort(freqs)}")
print(f"Detected freqs: {support}")
print(f"SNR FISTA only:       {snr(xtrue, xinv_fista):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR FISTA + debias:   {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote and ran `invert.py`. The reconstruction recovers the signal almost exactly: **SNR = 65.0 dB**, from 128 of the 512 samples.

**What I chose**
- **Basis:** Each term `cos(πf(2t+1)/(2n))` in your signal is exactly one basis function of the DCT-II (a type of discrete cosine transform). So the full signal has only 12 non-zero DCT coefficients out of 512. I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT`, which is orthonormal, so its adjoint (`D.H`) is its inverse.
- **Problem:** I solve `y = R · Dᴴ · p` for the coefficients `p`, assuming most of them are zero. The full signal is then `xinv = Dᴴ p`. Plain least squares can't do this because 128 equations for 512 unknowns has infinitely many solutions. Assuming `p` is sparse is what lets it work: the cosine basis spreads each component across all samples, while the recorded samples are randomly placed.
- **Solver:** I used `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista` (an L1-regularised solver), with `eps=1e-2` and 500 iterations.
- **Debiasing:** L1 solvers shrink the amplitudes they recover. So I kept the coefficients above 5% of the largest one and refit their amplitudes with ordinary least squares.

**How well it worked**
| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| FISTA alone | 54.3 dB |
| FISTA + debias (final `xinv`) | **65.0 dB** |

FISTA found exactly the 12 true frequencies (15, 48, 60, 62, 113, 120, 132, 151, 163, 172, 175, 178), with no false ones. The last printed line is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203583`, computed with `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)` on the reconstructed full signal.

**For your real data**
- This works so well because the synthetic cosines line up exactly with the DCT grid. Real periodic signals usually sit between grid frequencies, so their energy spreads over several coefficients. Expect a lower SNR, and you may need to tune `eps` or the debias threshold.
- If the 5% threshold gives a poor result on your real data, use the FISTA-only result instead, which is still good.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The problem is your adjoint. It applies the forward operator a second time instead of its transpose.

## Why

Your forward step is causal convolution, truncated to the first `nt` samples. Written as a matrix, that's lower-triangular Toeplitz: `y[i] = Σ_k h[k] x[i-k]`. The adjoint is the transpose, `x[j] = Σ_k h[k] y[j+k]`. That's an upper-triangular **cross-correlation**, which looks forward in time. Your `adjoint` still convolves (it looks backward in time), so it's the same operator again. CGLS assumes `Opᴴ` is the true adjoint. With this one its search directions are wrong, so it can't converge.

## Fix

Change one line:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[len(h) - 1:]
```

`np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]` is equivalent. With that change, your operator matches `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` exactly, forward and adjoint. You could use that built-in instead of a `FunctionOperator`.

## Results

I ran your script unchanged and then with the fix (PyLops 2.8.0, NumPy 2.0.2, SciPy 1.13.1):

| | dottest | cgls cost (iter 0 → 5 → 10 → last) | stop | relative error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Your adjoint | **FAIL** (`-7.94` vs `-19.23`) | 3.00 → 4.60 → 6.35 → **20.37** | ran all 100 iterations | **3.19** |
| Fixed adjoint | pass (`-50.876` both sides) | 3.00 → 0.78 → 0.38 → 0.0051 | tolerance reached at iteration 34 | **0.016** |

With your adjoint, the cost doesn't just stall: it rises over the iterations and the model error ends up above 300%. With the fix, the error drops to 1.6%. The operator is well conditioned (condition number ≈ 12), so once the adjoint is right there's no scaling or regularisation problem left. Your `damp=1e-3` with the 1e-3 noise level is fine.

I didn't check shapes, dtypes or NaNs separately. Everything here is real `float64`, 1-D, with matching `nt`, so I don't expect problems there, and the fixed run recovers the true model.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The problem is your `adjoint`: it isn't the adjoint of `forward`. It runs the same causal convolution a second time, so it computes A·y when CGLS needs Aᵀy.

**Why that's wrong.** Your forward operator is a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix, `y[n] = Σₖ h[n−k] x[k]`. Its transpose is upper-triangular, which makes it anti-causal. Applying Aᵀ means **cross-correlating** with `h`, i.e. convolving with the time-reversed filter `h[::-1]` and keeping the samples that line up with `x`:

```
xadj[k] = Σₙ h[n−k] y[n]
```

CGLS assumes the adjoint is exact. With a wrong one, it computes gradients that don't point downhill, the conjugacy between search directions breaks down, and the iterations stall. The failing `dottest` is the symptom of this.

**The fix:**

```python
nh = len(h)

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h[::-1], mode="full")[nh - 1 : nh - 1 + nt]
```

I ran your script with both versions:

| adjoint | dottest | relative error after 100 CGLS iterations |
|---|---|---|
| yours | fails | 3.19 |
| fixed | passes | 0.016 |

**A simpler option:** PyLops has this operator built in, with a correct adjoint:

```python
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=0)
```

With `offset=0`, `h[0]` is treated as time zero, so the filter is causal. I checked that it gives exactly the same output as your `forward` and that it passes `dottest`.

Whenever you write a `FunctionOperator` by hand, run `dottest` before inverting with it. Every Krylov solver in PyLops (`cgls`, `lsqr`, …) relies on the adjoint being exact.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Your `adjoint` function is wrong. It runs the same causal convolution as `forward`, so it's computing A·y when it should compute Aᵀ·y.

**Why:** Your forward operator is a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix, A[i,j] = h[i−j]. Its transpose is upper-triangular, so the adjoint has to be anti-causal: a cross-correlation with `h`, which runs backward in time:

```
(Aᵀy)[j] = Σ_k h[k] · y[j+k]
```

Because your adjoint isn't the real transpose, the dot test fails. CGLS assumes it gets the true Aᵀ, so with the wrong one its search directions and step lengths are wrong. That's why the cost stalls and the model ends up wrong.

**Fix:** To get the correlation, flip the input in time, convolve, truncate, then flip back:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

This is the same as `np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[len(h)-1 : len(h)-1+nt]`.

I ran your script with only this change:

```
Dot test passed, v^H(Opu)=62.85100999826369 - u^H(Op^Hv)=62.8510099982637
relative error: 0.0158
```

You could also skip writing the adjoint yourself and use PyLops' built-in operator, which gets the adjoint right:

```python
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=0)
```

With `offset=0`, `h[0]` is the zero-lag sample, so the filter is causal like yours.

**Tip:** Before running a solver on any `FunctionOperator`, run `dottest`. If it fails, the adjoint is wrong.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in your `adjoint`. It applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, but the adjoint of a convolution is a **correlation**, which runs the filter backward in time (anti-causal).

Your forward operator is

$$y[n] = \sum_k h[k]\,x[n-k], \quad n = 0..nt-1$$

so its adjoint is

$$x[k] = \sum_n h[n-k]\,y[n],$$

where each output sample gathers from *later* data samples. Your `adjoint` gathers from earlier samples instead. That function is the forward operator again, not its transpose. So `dottest` fails, and CGLS stalls because the gradients it computes, `Op.H @ r`, point in the wrong direction.

**Fix:**

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt]
```

An equivalent version reverses time, convolves causally, then reverses back:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

**Results from running your script both ways:**

| | dottest | relative model error |
|---|---|---|
| your adjoint | fails (3.90 vs −0.58) | 3.19 |
| corrected adjoint | passes | 0.016 |

**Tip:** PyLops has a built-in operator for this, which gives you a correct adjoint without writing one:

```python
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=0)
```

`offset=0` puts the filter's time zero at its first sample, so the convolution is causal like yours. Whichever way you build it, run `dottest` on a hand-written `FunctionOperator` before you invert with it.

Prompt

I wrapped my causal filter as a PyLops FunctionOperator to deconvolve with cgls, but cgls stalls: the cost barely decreases after the first few iterations and the model looks nothing like the truth. pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300) also fails. What's wrong with my code?

import numpy as np
import pylops

nt = 300
h = np.exp(-np.arange(30) / 6.0)  # causal, decaying filter

def forward(x):
    return np.convolve(x, h, mode="full")[:nt]

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h, mode="full")[:nt]

Op = pylops.FunctionOperator(forward, adjoint, nt, nt)

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
xtrue = np.zeros(nt)
xtrue[[40, 90, 150, 220]] = [1.0, -0.7, 0.5, 0.9]
y = Op @ xtrue + 1e-3 * rng.standard_normal(nt)

xinv = pylops.cgls(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(nt), niter=100, damp=1e-3)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is a causal convolution (np.convolve(x, h, "full")[:nt]), but the adjoint repeats the same convolution instead of correlating: the adjoint must use the time-reversed filter, e.g. np.convolve(y, h[::-1], "full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt] (or np.correlate(y, h, "full") with the matching slice), or the user can use pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D directly. With a correct adjoint cgls converges.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It says the adjoint is wrong (not the transpose of the forward) and that this is why the dot-test fails and cgls stalls.
  • It identifies the missing time reversal / correlation (and the corresponding slice) and gives a corrected adjoint equivalent to the ground truth, or recommends Convolve1D.
  • It does not name the solver choice, damp, niter or noise as the main cause.

Otherwise FAIL.

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ba0b104e-27ca-40d1-8127-2ec0f4f6ca25","uuid":"c209c3ed-5860-420f-ac9d-371f41e8c20f","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:50:11.010Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_013UnJ2kcTCRxvtxp7fMTKkq","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i], xnorm[i], \"o\", label=name)\n    axs[0].set_xlabel(\"||y - Op x||\")\n    axs[0].set_ylabel(\"||R x||\")\n    axs[0].set_title(\"L-curve\")\n    axs[0].legend()\n    axs[1].loglog(epsilons, rno

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"269a8032-c4b7-46cd-9342-ee70bb27bff6","uuid":"f45d98db-9f79-4937-b889-50cc62c08963","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:50:15.227Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0118yNBXjecXqhY6VdbpP2iE","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i], xnorm[i], \"o\", label=name)\n    axs[0].set_xlabel(\"||y - Op x||\")\n    axs[0].set_ylabel(\"||R x||\")\n    axs[0].set_title(\"L-curve\")\n    axs[0].legend()\n    axs[1].lo

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"3afbdc4a-cbf3-4c7b-a9f5-48a8ba37b716","uuid":"19595cb0-a944-40f4-ba0f-9a5c91e142ce","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:50:20.305Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Q4NFYsF6tt3DjraH3kxxxa","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i], xnorm[i], \"o\", label=name)\n    axs[0].set_xlabel(\"||y - Op x||\")\n    axs[0].set_ylabel(\"||R x||\")\n    axs[0].set_title(\"L-curve\")\n    axs[0].legend

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/e-x4pXsf/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.295/6e740263fc21262bc60937623e9faa80/dataviz\n\n# Data Visualization\n\nA chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns \"make it look\ngood\" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather\nthan by taste.\n\n**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form\nheuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design\nsystem supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a\ndiverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);\nthe method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the\nreference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your\nbrand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.\n\n> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**\n> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.\n\n## The procedure - do these in order\n\nColor comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.\n\n1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a\n   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes\n   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`\n2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential\n   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.\n   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`\n3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**\n   `node scripts/validate_palette.js \"<hex,hex,...>\" --mode light` (relative to\n   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type=\"module\">` in the\n   chart's own page, where it reads\n   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns\n   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,\n   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for\n   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.\n4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to\n   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked\n   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,\n   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`\n5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship\n   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.\n   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger\n   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`\n6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4\n   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names\n   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own\n   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic\n   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.\n7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or\n   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow\n   before calling it done.\n\nThen check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog\nof what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.\n\n## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)\n\n- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a\n  generated hue - it folds into \"Other,\" small multiples, or composite encoding.\n- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different\n  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the\n  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*\n- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series\n  count must not repaint the survivors.\n- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray\n  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.\n- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the\n  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A\n  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the\n  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse\n  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN\n  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.\n- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with\n  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**\n- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends\n  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.\n- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused\n  for \"series 4\"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.\n\n## Plugging in a design system\n\nThe method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference\ninstance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.\n\n| Parameter | What the system provides |\n|---|---|\n| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |\n| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |\n| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |\n| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |\n| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |\n| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |\n| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |\n| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |\n\nTo onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let\nit snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.\n\n## Reference files\n\n| File | What it answers |\n|------|-----------------|\n| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |\n| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |\n| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |\n| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |\n| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of - build each in plain HTML |\n| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong - check every chart against this** |\n| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** - every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |\n| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"509bc534-50d4-4a89-8d4a-a77900b9f9d5","uuid":"44f588ed-c2f6-4a40-9f10-b10893a24caa","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:50:32.180Z","isSynthetic":true}
