Use **`epsRs=[3.0]`** (the corner is at ε ≈ 3.2). Going lower makes the result worse. Plot saved to `lcurve.png`:\n\n![L-curve](lcurve.png)\n\n**How I chose it**\n\nI ran your inversion for 46 values of ε from 1e-3 to ~30. For each one I recorded the data misfit ‖y − Op x‖ and the roughness ‖D2 x‖, and plotted them on log–log axes. Your setup gives a very sharp \"L\":\n\n- **Vertical arm (ε < 1):** the misfit barely improves (0.250 → 0.230), but roughness jumps from 0.08 to 27. That is the solver fitting noise.\n- **Horizontal arm (ε > 5):** roughness barely drops, but the misfit grows quickly. That is over-smoothing.\n- **Corner:** I found it as the point of maximum curvature, at **ε ≈ 3.2**. Anything from about 2 to 4 is effectively the same.\n\n**Cross-checks**\n\n| Method | ε | Relative error vs `xtrue` |\n|---|---|---|\n| Your choice | 1.0 | 0.0191 |\n| **L-curve corner** | **3.16** | **0.0152** |\n| Best possible (uses `xtrue`, so only possible on synthetic data) | 2.5 | 0.0143 |\n| Discrepancy principle (‖r‖ ≈ σ√n = 0.283) | 7.9 | 0.0274 |\n\n- The corner lands almost exactly on the true-error minimum (right panel), so the L-curve works well here.\n- The discrepancy principle is the other standard method when you know the noise level. Here it over-smooths a little, which is common for it.\n- `iter_lim=200` is enough: the results match a direct dense least-squares solve to 4 digits at every ε, so the curve isn't affected by early stopping.\n\n**About \"too smooth\"**\n\nThe numbers don't back that up. ε=1 actually under-regularizes slightly, and its error is about 30% higher than ε≈2.5–3. If the result still looks too smooth to you, the cause is probably the **regularizer**, not ε. The second-derivative penalty flattens the narrow Gaussian bump at z=120, whatever the weight. Lowering ε won't sharpen the bump; it only lets noise through. If preserving sharp features matters, try an edge-preserving regularizer (TV or L1, e.g. `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`) rather than tuning ε down.\n\n**Notes**\n\n- `epsRs` multiplies the operator, so the penalty is ε²‖D2 x‖². That's why the useful range spans orders of magnitude, and why you should always sweep on a log scale.\n- On real data, rerun this sweep whenever the noise level, operator or model size changes. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/e-NCqHGc/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.295/a51c9c87e8aafaad5f9cc373433324a4/dataviz\n\n# Data Visualization\n\nA chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns \"make it look\ngood\" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather\nthan by taste.\n\n**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form\nheuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design\nsystem supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a\ndiverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);\nthe method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the\nreference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your\nbrand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.\n\n> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**\n> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.\n\n## The procedure - do these in order\n\nColor comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.\n\n1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a\n   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes\n   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`\n2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential\n   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.\n   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`\n3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**\n   `node scripts/validate_palette.js \"<hex,hex,...>\" --mode light` (relative to\n   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type=\"module\">` in the\n   chart's own page, where it reads\n   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns\n   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,\n   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for\n   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.\n4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to\n   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked\n   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,\n   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`\n5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship\n   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.\n   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger\n   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`\n6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4\n   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names\n   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own\n   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic\n   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.\n7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or\n   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow\n   before calling it done.\n\nThen check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog\nof what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.\n\n## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)\n\n- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a\n  generated hue - it folds into \"Other,\" small multiples, or composite encoding.\n- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different\n  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the\n  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*\n- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series\n  count must not repaint the survivors.\n- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray\n  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.\n- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the\n  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A\n  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the\n  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse\n  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN\n  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.\n- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with\n  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**\n- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends\n  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.\n- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused\n  for \"series 4\"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.\n\n## Plugging in a design system\n\nThe method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference\ninstance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.\n\n| Parameter | What the system provides |\n|---|---|\n| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |\n| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |\n| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |\n| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |\n| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |\n| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |\n| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |\n| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |\n\nTo onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let\nit snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.\n\n## Reference files\n\n| File | What it answers |\n|------|-----------------|\n| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |\n| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |\n| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |\n| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |\n| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of - build each in plain HTML |\n| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong - check every chart against this** |\n| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** - every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |\n| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a953347e-3e09-4ce3-a869-74c81ed81258","uuid":"39e911e5-a497-4ee0-bc5e-0e9cfe428cfd","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:51:20.636Z","isSynthetic":true}

Moving to about 3 cuts the error by roughly 20%. Since this is synthetic data I could check against `xtrue`: the best possible value (≈2.5) is right next to the L-curve corner.\n- **The \"too smooth\" look probably isn't from `epsRs`.** A 9-sample-wide Gaussian blur plus noise permanently removes fine detail, so no regularization weight brings it back. The Gaussian bump at z=120 has σ=6, close to the blur width, so it will always look a bit rounded. To get sharper features you'd need a different regularizer, such as total variation (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`) or a first-derivative penalty, not a smaller `epsRs`.\n- **Near the optimum the choice is forgiving.** The error curve is fairly flat between about 1 and 5, so anything in that range is reasonable.\n\n## For real data, where you have no `xtrue`\n- **L-curve corner:** works without knowing the noise level. Sweep on a log grid and take the point of maximum curvature. This is the default I'd use.\n- **Discrepancy principle:** pick the `epsRs` where ‖y − Op x‖ ≈ σ√n. It needs the noise level σ, and it tends to over-smooth slightly, as it did here (7.9).\n- **Scale:** PyLops minimizes ‖y − Op x‖² + ε²‖D2 x‖², so `epsRs` enters squared. Always sweep it on a log scale, and redo the sweep whenever you change the operator's scaling or the data amplitude.\n\nChange your line to:\n```python\nxinv = pylops.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[3.0], iter_lim=200)[0]\n```\nAt `epsRs=3`, 200 iterations is enough to converge.","ttft_ms":3909,"type":"result","duration_ms":62494,"uuid":"a06f2c6a-b7ff-499e-af0a-66eedb69c715","ttft_stream_ms":1139,"time_to_request_ms":40,"first_content_frame_ms":1139,"queued_turn_count":0,"result_index":0}

The misfit hardly changes (0.250 → 0.246) while the roughness grows quickly, so the solver is starting to fit noise. ε = 1 sits on the bend, a little on this side of it.\n- **Right of the corner (ε ≳ 5):** the misfit rises fast for very little extra smoothness. Beyond this point you start to smear real structure.\n- **Agreement with the true error:** the corner lands close to the ε that minimises the true error. That's a good sign the L-curve can be trusted for this problem when you don't have `xtrue`.\n- **Discrepancy principle:** it suggests ε ≈ 8 here, which over-smooths. If you know the noise level, it's still a useful upper bound.\n\n**Why ε = 1 might look too smooth:** probably because of the Gaussian bump at z ≈ 120. Second-derivative regularisation flattens sharp features like it at every ε, and more so as ε grows: the peak error is +0.007 at ε = 1 and −0.029 at ε = 3. A larger ε still gives the lower overall error, because it removes noise-driven wiggles everywhere else. If getting that bump's amplitude right matters more than overall error, the fix is a different regulariser, such as a blocky or sparsity-promoting one, not a smaller ε. Also, in `regularized_inversion` the ε multiplies D2 inside the least-squares objective, so it acts as ε² in the cost. That's why the useful range spans orders of magnitude and the sweep should be log-spaced.\n\n`iter_lim=200` is enough: at these ε values the results match a fully converged solve to about 1e-6.\n\n**Files in your working directory:**\n- `lcurve_sweep.py` runs the ε sweep and prints the corner, the discrepancy-principle value, the best ε and the full table.\n- `lcurve_plot.py` makes the figure. It reads the sweep results from `$TMPDIR/lc.npz`, so run the sweep script first.\n- `lcurve.png` is the figure above.","ttft_ms":3843,"type":"result","duration_ms":99081,"uuid":"b1439cda-1b90-4498-b917-01570e8c87d7","ttft_stream_ms":1150,"time_to_request_ms":27,"first_content_frame_ms":1151,"queued_turn_count":0,"result_index":0}

Skill called 0x (expected 0..0)

Prompt

My smooth-model inversion with PyLops works fine, but I picked epsRs=[1.0] by eye and the result looks a bit too smooth. How should I choose epsRs properly? Please make me an L-curve for it and tell me which value to use. Here's the script:

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(5)
n = 200
z = np.arange(n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * z / 80) + 0.5 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 120) / 6) ** 2)
t = np.arange(-15, 16)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 4) ** 2); h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=15)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.02 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)
xinv = pylops.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[1.0], iter_lim=200)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))

  • The answer (with a script that was actually run in the trace) sweeps epsRs over a logarithmic range of values (at least ~8 values spanning several orders of magnitude) with the user's regularized_inversion setup.
  • For each value it computes the data residual norm and the regularisation norm (||D2 x||), and builds an L-curve (plot saved to a file and/or the values tabulated); it selects a value by a stated criterion (L-curve corner / maximum curvature, and/or the discrepancy principle with noise std 0.02).
  • It reports the recommended epsRs and the numbers backing it, and they do not visibly contradict the tool output in the trace (parts of the trace may be elided; do not fail for numbers you cannot find).
  • It keeps the user's solver and regulariser rather than re-designing the inversion (a brief side remark about alternatives is fine).

Otherwise FAIL.
